Representations of the rational Cherednik algebra H t , c ​ ( S 3 , 𝔥 ) H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h}) in positive characteristic
Martina Balagović and Jordan Barnes

Abstract
. We study the rational Cherednik algebra H t , c ​ ( S 3 , 𝔥 ) H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h}) of type A 2 A_{2} in positive characteristic p p , and its irreducible category 𝒪 \mathcal{O} representations L t , c ​ ( τ ) L_{t,c}(\tau) . For every possible value of p , t , c p,t,c , and τ \tau we calculate the Hilbert polynomial and the character of L t , c ​ ( τ ) L_{t,c}(\tau) , and give explicit generators of the maximal proper graded submodule of the Verma module.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The algebra

For a general overview of rational Cherednik algebras and their representation see
[
EM11
]
; for specifics in positive characteristic see
[
BCb
]
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field of positive characteristic
p
, and
\mathbb{F}_{p}=\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
its finite subfield with
p
elements. Consider the symmetric group
S_{3}
, let
V
be its permutation representation with a basis
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}
and the action
g.y_{i}=y_{g(i)}
,
\mathfrak{h}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}y_{i}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}=0\right\}
its two dimensional subrepresentation (the standard representation),
V^{*}
the dual of
V
with the dual basis
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
the dual of
\mathfrak{h}
, realised as a quotient of
V^{*}
and spanned by
\overline{x_{1}}
,
\overline{x_{2}}
,
\overline{x_{3}}
satisfying
\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{3}}=0
. Let
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>
denote the evaluation pairings
V^{*}\otimes V\to\Bbbk
(satisfying
\left<x_{i},y_{j}\right>=\delta_{ij}
) and
\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}\to\Bbbk
. The group
S_{3}
is a reflection group with
\mathfrak{h}
its reflection representation, so we can associate a rational Cherednik algebra to it.

Let
t,c\in\Bbbk
be parameters. Let
\Bbbk[S_{3}]
be the group algebra of
S_{3}
over
\Bbbk
and
T(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{*})
the tensor algebra of
\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. The rational Cherednik algebra
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
is the quotient of
\Bbbk[S_{3}]\ltimes T(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{*})
by the relations (for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{h}
,
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
)

(1)

[x,x^{\prime}]=0,\quad[y,y^{\prime}]=0,\quad[y,x]=t\left<x,y\right>-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}c\left<x-(ij).x,y\right>(ij).

The algebra
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
is graded by
\deg x=1
,
\deg y=-1
and
\deg g=0
. It has a triangular decomposition in the sense that the group algebra
\Bbbk[S_{3}]
and the symmetric algebras
S\mathfrak{h}
and
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
are subalgebras, and the multiplication map

S\mathfrak{h}\otimes\Bbbk[S_{3}]\otimes S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\to H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})

is a vector space isomorphism. Let
S=\left\{(12),(23),(13)\right\}

be the set of reflections in
S_{3}
.

1.2.
The modules

Let
\tau
be an irreducible representation of
S_{3}
, and augment it to a
\Bbbk[S_{3}]\ltimes S\mathfrak{h}
module by setting the action of
\mathfrak{h}
on
\tau
to be
0
. The Verma module
M_{t,c}(\tau)
is the induced module

M_{t,c}(\tau)=H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})\otimes_{\Bbbk[S_{3}]\ltimes S\mathfrak{h}}\tau.

As a vector space
M_{t,c}(\tau)\cong S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
, with the action of
g\in S_{3}
on
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
being diagonal, the action of
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
being by multiplication on the first tensor factor, and the action of
y\in\mathfrak{h}
given by the Dunkl operators

D_{y}=t\partial_{y}\otimes\mathrm{id}-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}c\left<x_{i}-x_{j},y\right>\frac{1-(ij)}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\otimes(ij).

Setting
\deg(\tau)=0
makes
M_{t,c}(\tau)
into a graded representation of
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
, and this grading coincides with the natural grading on
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
.

In positive characteristic
M_{t,c}(\tau)
has a maximal proper graded submodule
J_{t,c}(\tau)
, and the quotient
L_{t,c}(\tau)=M_{t,c}(\tau)/J_{t,c}(\tau)
is irreducible and finite dimensional. Category
\mathcal{O}
(denoted
\mathcal{O}_{t,c}
or
\mathcal{O}_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
when necessary) is the category of all finite dimensional graded
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
modules. The morphisms are all
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
-maps which preserve the grading. For a graded module
M=\oplus_{k}M^{k}
and an integer
l
, the shifted module
M[l]
has the same underlying vector space and the algebra action and the grading
M[l]^{k}=M^{l+k}
. Irreducible modules in category
\mathcal{O}_{t,c}
up to isomorphism are
L_{t,c}(\tau)[k]
for all irreducible representations
\tau
of
S_{3}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

1.3.
The main questions

We wish to describe all
L_{t,c}(\tau)
in three ways:

(1)

By calculating their Hilbert polynomials. For a graded vector space
M=\oplus_{k}M^{k}
with finite dimensional graded pieces
M^{k}
, its Hilbert series is

h_{M}(z)=\sum_{k}\dim M^{k}z^{k}\in\mathbb{Z}[z,z^{-1}].

(2)

By calculating their characters. Let
K_{0}(S_{3})
be the Grothendieck group of
S_{3}
representations over
\Bbbk
, and for an
S_{3}
representation
M
let
[M]
be its class in
K_{0}(S_{3})
. For a graded
S_{3}
representation
M=\oplus_{k}M^{k}
with finite dimensional graded pieces
M^{k}
, its character is

\chi_{M}(z)=\sum_{k}[M^{k}]z^{k}\in K_{0}(S_{3})[z,z^{-1}].

(3)

By specifying a finite set of generators of the maximal proper graded submodule
J_{t,c}(\tau)
. One can always find such a set by considering singular vectors. A vector
v
in a graded
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
module is called
singular
if it is homogeneous, not of lowest degree, and satisfies
y.v=0
for all
y\in\mathfrak{h}
. Every such vector generates a proper graded subrepresentation. To find a set of generators of
J_{t,c}(\tau)
, we first look for singular vectors in
M_{t,c}(\tau)
, take a quotient of
M_{t,c}(\tau)
by the submodule they generate, then look for singular vectors in this quotient, and iterate this process until we get an irreducible module. (It always terminates.)

Notice that (1), (2) and (3) above are not independent, as each item carries strictly more information than the previous one. Knowing the generators of
J_{t,c}(\tau)
usually makes it possible to find the characters
\chi_{L_{t,c}(\tau)}(z)
, and from there it is straightforward to compute the Hilbert polynomials
h_{L_{t,c}(\tau)}(z)
.

Once the above are known for
L_{t,c}(\tau)
, they are easily computed for
L_{t,c}(\tau)[k]
by shifting degrees; for example, the Hilbert series satisfy

h_{M[l]}(z)=z^{-l}h_{M}(z).

1.4.
The parameters

In this paper we aim to answer (1), (2) and (3) for
L_{t,c}(\tau)
for all
t,c,\tau
and
p
for which they are previously unknown. As the group
S_{3}
has order
3!=6
, the representation theory of
S_{3}
is semisimple with three irreducible representations
\tau=\mathrm{Triv},\mathrm{Sign},\mathrm{Stand}
when
p>3
, not semisimple with two irreducible representations
\tau=\mathrm{Triv},\mathrm{Sign}
when
p=3
, and not semisimple with two irreducible representations
\tau=\mathrm{Triv},\mathrm{Stand}
when
p=2
(see Section
2.1
). The parameter
t\in\Bbbk
can be assumed to be
0
or
1
, leading to different behaviours (see Lemma
2.14
). The parameter
c
takes all values in
\Bbbk
. When
t=0
, the irreducible modules
L_{0,c}(\tau)
behave in one way when
c\neq 0
(generic values) and in a different way when
c=0
(special value). When
t=1
, the irreducible modules
L_{1,c}(\tau)
behave in one way when
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{p}
(generic values) and in a different way when
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
(special values) - see Lemma
2.16
and the paragraph above it.

1.5.
History and related work

Rational Cherednik algebras were introduced in 2002 by Pavel Etingof and Victor Ginzburg
[
EG
]
, building on earlier work of Ivan Cherednik
[
Cheb
,
Chea
]
. Since then, their representation theory has been studied extensively. A lot of work takes place over fields of characteristic 0, see
[
DJO
,
BEGb
,
BEGa
,
CE
,
GGOR
,
Gor
,
Dun
,
Gri08
]
. Less is known in positive characteristic, see
[
Lat
,
BFG
,
BM13
,
BCb
,
BCa
,
DSa
,
DSb
,
CK
]
.

Rational Cherednik algebras associated to the reflection group
S_{n}
in characteristic
0
have been studied in
[
BFG
]
and
[
Gor
]
. The setup for the representation theory of rational Cherednik algebra in positive characteristics has been described in
[
BCb
]
.

The preprint
[
Lia12
]
proves several results about rational Cherednik algebras associated to the symmetric and dihedral groups. Most importantly, it clarifies that for
S_{n}
, the parameter
c
being generic means
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{p}
. The setup uses
V
instead of
\mathfrak{h}
for the reflection representation, but the Hilbert polynomials, characters, and singular vectors can be translated, see Proposition
2.2
. This paper calculates the Hilbert polynomial of
L_{1,0}(\mathrm{Triv})
,
L_{1,3^{-1}}(\mathrm{Triv})
when
p\neq 2
, describes the singular vectors in the case
p>3
,
t=1
,
\tau=\mathrm{Triv}
,
c\in\left\{0,1,2,\ldots,p-1\right\}
with
0<c<p/2
or
2p/3<c<p
, and conjectures the degrees of the singular vectors when
p/2<c<2p/3
. We prove their conjecture. The previously known cases are marked
[
Lia12
]
in the tables below.

The paper
[
DSa
]
considers rational Cherednik algebras associated to complex reflection groups
G(m,r,n)
in positive characteristic and their representation theory for generic values of the parameter
c
. Its Section 4 provides characters for
G=G(m,1,n)
in the non-modular case, which overlaps with our work when
m=1,n=3
,
p>3
. The characters calculated are for all
L_{t,c}(\tau)
with
p>n
,
t=0,1
,
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and all
\tau
. Those cases are marked
[
DSa
]
in the tables below, and our contribution in those cases is the calculation of singular vectors. (These singular vectors are later used in the description of
L_{t,c}(\tau)
with
p>3
,
t=0,1
,
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and all
\tau
, which is new).

The paper
[
DSb
]
examines the characteristic
p
representation theory of rational Cherednik algebras associated to
S_{n}
in the case that
p\mid n
,
\tau=\mathrm{Triv}
,
t=1
, providing singular vectors and the Hilbert polynomials. This is relevant for us in the case
p=3
,
\tau=\mathrm{Triv}
,
t=1
, marked
[
DSb
]
in the tables below.

The paper
[
CK
]
studies the rational Cherednik algebra
H_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h})
for generic
c
and its representation theory in characteristic
p\mid{n-1}
. This only coincides with our work for
n=3
,
p=2
,
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{2}
, where they calculate the Hilbert polynomials of
L_{t,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
. We mark this case by
[
CK
]
in the table below.

1.6.
Methods

We extensively use the standard computational methods: the grading Casimir element
\Omega
(Lemma
2.1
), equivalences of categories for algebras obtained by rescaling the parameters (Lemmas
2.14
and
2.15
), the fact that for generic
c
interesting things only happen in degrees divisible by
p
(Proposition 3.4. in
[
BCb
]
), information about which
c
are generic (Proposition
2.16
), and general information about some special values of
c
(Lemmas
2.17
,
2.18
,
2.19
). In early stages of the project we used the computational software Magma
[
BCP
]
to generate conjectures, guess singular vectors, and check the Hilbert polynomials of
L_{t,c}(\tau)
for specific
\tau,t,c,p
.

A new computational method we use is to write
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
in the Young basis (see Section
2.3
), and use this and our understanding of symmetric polynomials to choose a good basis for
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
M_{t,c}(\tau)\cong S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
(see Theorems
2.4
,
2.6
,
2.10
, and
2.12
). Such a basis is useful when looking for singular vectors in
M_{t,c}(\tau)
and its quotients because:

i)

It is compatible with the decomposition into
S_{3}
subrepresentations. When looking for singular vectors, the Casimir element
\Omega
(Lemma
2.1
) can tell us which isotypic components we need to examine in which degrees, and this basis makes it easy to restrict to a particular isotypic component in some degree. This reduces the size of the space we are examining for singular vectors.

ii)

It is compatible with the restriction to
S_{2}
, in the sense that every vector is either
S_{2}
-symmetric or skew symmetric. A consequence is that we can reduce the search for singular vectors (which are in the kernel of all Dunkl operators
D_{y}
) to the search for vectors in the kernel of
D_{y_{1}}
, see Lemma
2.20
.

iii)

Every vector in it is a product of an invariant from
(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{3}}
and one of the small number of fixed vectors of small degree. The difference parts of Dunkl operators are
(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{3}}
-linear and the differential parts of Dunkl operators are compatible with products (Leibniz rule). This makes the Dunkl operator calculations manageable.

Using these properties to reduce the number of computations we need to do, we calculate the values of Dunkl operators explicitly and look for singular vectors. This lets us describe the generators of
J_{t,c}(\tau)
, from where we compute the characters and the Hilbert polynomials of
L_{t,c}(\tau)
.

p=2
, Theorem

describes
L_{t,c}(\tau)
for all
t,c,\tau
by giving the generators of
J_{t,c}(\tau)

p>3
and generic

[

calculate the characters and the Hilbert polynomials of all
L_{t,c}(\tau)
. We describe the generators of
J_{t,c}(\tau)
(for general interest, and to use in describing
L_{t,c}(\tau)
for special
c
), and along the way we reprove their results in this case. This can be found in Theorem
6.1
.

For characteristic
p>3
and special
c
, see Theorem
7.1
. The case
c=0
is standard and well known. For
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
,
[
Lia12
]
describes
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
except when
p/2<c<2p/3
, where they give conjectured degrees of the generators. We deal with this case, finding generators of
J_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
, characters, and Hilbert polynomials. This finishes the description of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
. The description of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Sign})
follows by Lemma
2.15
.

The description of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
for
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
,
0<c<p/3
is done by finding the generators of
J_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
, characters, and Hilbert polynomials. We are only able to prove this using a technical Assumption
7.14
on the field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. This assumption holds for all primes
p<2023
, and we conjecture it holds for all primes. The description of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
for
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
,
2p/3<c<p
follows by Lemma
2.15
.

The description of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
for
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
,
p/3<c<p/2
, is conjectural, see Conjecture
7.25
, and the description of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
for
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
,
p/2<c<2p/3
would follow from there. We checked these conjectures in Magma
[
BCP
]
for primes up to
20
.

1.8.
Further questions

Obvious follow up questions are to prove Conjecture
7.25
, and show that Assumption
7.14
holds for all primes
p
. It would be interesting to see if similar methods can be used beyond
S_{3}
, most interestingly for special
c
and representations other than the trivial. This could help gather evidence for a conjecture by Etingof and Reins that the representation theory of
H_{t,c}(S_{n})
in characteristic
p
in a certain sense only depends on
n\bmod p
.

1.9.
Roadmap of the paper

In Section
2
we list all the preliminaries, including both the standard tools in Category
\mathcal{O}
and the new results about the bases of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
,
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
M_{t,c}(\tau)
which we will use. In Section
3
we completely describe all
L_{t,c}(\tau)
when
p=2
, and in Section
4
all
L_{t,c}(\tau)
when
p=3
. Section
5
contains many explicit auxiliary computations we will need to fully use the basis of
M_{t,c}(\tau)
in case
p>3
. We tackle the cases
p>3
in Section
6
(generic
c
) and Section
7
(special
c
).

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Irreducible representations of
S_{3}
in positive characteristic

We will use
s_{1}=(12)
and
s_{2}=(23)
to denote the generators of the symmetric group
S_{3}
. Over algebraically closed fields of characteristic
0
the group
S_{3}
has three finite dimensional irreducible representations up to isomorphism: the trivial, the sign and the standard representation. The notation we use for them and their character table is given below.

S_{3}

e

(12)

(123)

1

3

2

\mathrm{Triv}

1

1

1

\mathrm{Sign}

1

-1

1

\mathrm{Stand}

2

0

-1

Considering its Brauer characters over an algebraically closed field
\Bbbk
of characteristic
p>0
we immediately see that:

•

if
p=2
,
\mathrm{Triv}=\mathrm{Sign}
, and irreducible representations are
\mathrm{Triv}
and
\mathrm{Stand}
;

•

if
p=3
,
\mathrm{Stand}
is not irreducible, and irreducible representations are
\mathrm{Triv}
and
\mathrm{Sign}
;

•

if
p>3
, irreducible representations are
\mathrm{Triv}
,
\mathrm{Sign}
and
\mathrm{Stand}
, and the category of representations of
S_{3}
over
\Bbbk
is semisimple.

The following calculation works for arbitrary characteristic, though in some characteristics (namely,
2
and
3
) the resulting scalars coincide.

Lemma 2.1
.

Consider the Casimir element
\Omega
in
H_{1,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
, given by

\Omega=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overline{x_{i}}(y_{i}-y_{3})+c\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}(1-(ij)).

Assume
M
is a graded
H_{1,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
-module and
\tau\subseteq M
an irreducible
S_{3}
-subrepresentation contained in the kernel of all Dunkl operators. Then
\Omega
acts on
\tau
by a constant as follows:

\Omega|_{\mathrm{Triv}}=0\cdot\mathrm{id},\quad\Omega|_{\mathrm{Sign}}=6c\cdot\mathrm{id},\quad\Omega|_{\mathrm{Stand}}=3c\cdot\mathrm{id}.

Proof.

Standard direct computation; the term
\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overline{x_{i}}(y_{i}-y_{3})
acts by
0
on any vector in the kernel of Dunkl operators, and the term
\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}(1-(ij))
is central in
\Bbbk[S_{3}]
so acts on any irreducible
\tau
by a constant which can be calculated from the characters.
∎

2.2.
Reflection representations of
S_{n}
in positive characteristic

The group
S_{n}
has a natural permutation representation
V
with a basis
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
and the action given on this basis as
g.y_{i}=y_{g(i)}
. It is self dual, with
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
the dual basis of
V^{*}
and the isomorphism
V\to V^{*}
given by
y_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
.

Let
Y=y_{1}+\ldots+y_{n}
and
X=x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}
. They span
\Bbbk Y\subseteq V
and
\Bbbk X\subseteq V^{*}
, which are one dimensional subrepresentations isomorphic to the trivial representation
\mathrm{Triv}
. Another subrepresentation of
V
is

\mathfrak{h}=\left\{\sum_{i}a_{i}y_{i}\mid\sum a_{i}=0\right\}.

It is called the standard representation and we denote it
\mathrm{Stand}
.

When the characteristic
p
of the field
\Bbbk
does not divide
n
,
\Bbbk Y
and
\mathfrak{h}
are in direct sum,
V=\Bbbk Y\oplus\mathfrak{h}
, and
\mathfrak{h}
is irreducible. The dual is then also a direct sum,
V^{*}=\Bbbk X\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. Here

\Bbbk X\cong(\Bbbk Y)^{*}=\left\{x\in V^{*}\mid\left<x,y\right>=0\quad\forall y\in\mathfrak{h}\right\}

with
\left<X,Y\right>=n\neq 0
, and
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
can be realised as

V^{*}/\Bbbk X=\mathrm{span}\left\{\overline{x_{i}}=x_{i}+\Bbbk X\right\}

or as

\left\{x\in V^{*}\mid\left<x,Y\right>=0\right\}=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{i}-x_{j}\right\}\subseteq V^{*}.

The isomorphism between these two realisations is given by the splitting

\pi:V^{*}/\Bbbk X\hookrightarrow V^{*}\qquad\pi(\overline{x_{i}})=x_{i}-\frac{1}{n}X.

When the characteristic
p
of the field
\Bbbk
divides
n
,
Y
lies in
\mathfrak{h}
so
\Bbbk
and
\mathfrak{h}
are not in direct sum and
\mathfrak{h}
is not irreducible. In that case there is a non-split exact sequence

0\to\mathfrak{h}\to V\to V/\mathfrak{h}\to 0,

with
V/\mathfrak{h}\cong\mathrm{Triv}
. Dualising we get another non-split exact sequence

0\to(V/\mathfrak{h})^{*}\to V\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}\to 0,

with

(V/\mathfrak{h})^{*}=\left\{x\in V^{*}\mid\left<x,\mathfrak{h}\right>=0\right\}=\Bbbk X,

so in this case

\mathfrak{h}^{*}=V^{*}/\Bbbk X=\mathrm{span}\left\{\overline{x_{i}}=x_{i}+\Bbbk X\right\}.

In any characteristic both the representation
V
and its subrepresentation
\mathfrak{h}
are reflection representations, so one can consider the rational Cherednik algebras
H_{t,c}(S_{n},V)
and
H_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h})
. The two choices lead to slightly different conventions and computations in the literature, though the two algebras and their representations are very closely related. For example, we have the following lemma.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field whose finite characteristic
p
does not divide
n
,
\tau
an irreducible representation of
S_{n}
,

L_{t,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)
the irreducible representation with lowest weight
\tau
for the rational Cherednik algebra
H_{t,c}(S_{n},V)
over
\Bbbk
generated by
V,V^{*},S_{n}
, and
L_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)
the irreducible representation with lowest weight
\tau
for the rational Cherednik algebra
H_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h})
over
\Bbbk
generated by
\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{*},S_{n}
. Their characters are related as follows:

•

if
t=0
,

\chi_{L_{0,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)}(z)=\chi_{L_{0,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)}(z)

•

if
t=1
,

\chi_{L_{1,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)}(z)=\chi_{L_{1,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)}(z)\cdot\left(\frac{1-z^{p}}{1-z}\right).

Proof.

The representation
L_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)
can be written as a quotient of the Verma module
M_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)
by a submodule
\left<v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right>
generated by some homogeneous vectors
v_{i}
of degrees
d_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. We can assume without loss of generality that
0<d_{1}\leq d_{2}\leq\ldots\leq d_{k}
, and for every
i
and all
y\in\mathfrak{h}
we have
D_{y}(v_{i})\in\left<v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1}\right>
.

As
p
does not divide
n
, we have
V^{*}\cong\mathfrak{h}^{*}\oplus\Bbbk X
. Using the inclusion
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\subseteq SV^{*}
, we can consider
v_{i}\in M_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)\cong S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
as elements of
M_{t,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)\subseteq SV^{*}\otimes\tau
. We claim that

\displaystyle L_{0,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)

\displaystyle=M_{0,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)/\left<X\otimes\tau,v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right>,

\displaystyle L_{1,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)

\displaystyle=M_{1,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)/\left<X^{p}\otimes\tau,v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right>.

First, let us show that for every
i
and all
y\in V
we have
D_{y}(v_{i})\in\left<v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1}\right>
. If
y\in\mathfrak{h}\subseteq V
this is true by assumption, so it remains to check it for
y=Y
. This follows from the fact that
[Y,x]=0
for all
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and that
v_{i}\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
.

Next, note that when
t=0
we have
[y,X]=0
for all
y\in V
, and when
t=1
we have
[y,X^{p}]=0
for all
y\in V
. As a consequence, for
t=0
the set
X\otimes\tau
consists of singular vectors in degree
1
, and when
t=1
the set
X^{p}\otimes\tau
consists of singular vectors in degree
p
.

Let
J_{0}=\left<X\otimes\tau,v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right>
for
t=0
and
J_{1}=\left<X^{p}\otimes\tau,v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right>
for
t=1
. The above shows that
J_{t}
is a proper graded submodule of
M_{t,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)
, and so
L_{t,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)
is a quotient of
M_{t,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)/J_{t}
.

Finally, let us show that the module
M_{t,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)/J_{t}
is irreducible. If it is not, then there is a homogeneous vector
v\in M^{m}_{t,c}(S_{n},V,\tau)
, which is not in
J_{t}
, and is such that for all
y\in V
we have
D_{y}(v)\in J_{t}
. Write
v\in SV^{*}\otimes\tau\cong\Bbbk[X]\otimes S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
as

v=\sum_{i=0}^{m}X^{m-i}f_{i}

for
f_{i}\in S^{i}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
. The condition
D_{y_{j}}(v)\in J_{t}
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
then becomes

\displaystyle D_{y_{j}}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m}[y_{j},X^{m-i}]f_{i}+X^{m-i}D_{y_{j}}(f_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m}(m-i)X^{m-i-1}f_{i}+X^{m-i}D_{y_{j}}(f_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left((m-i+1)X^{m-i}f_{i-1}+X^{m-i}D_{y_{j}}(f_{i})\right)\in J_{t}.

This can be rewritten as

X^{m-i}D_{y_{j}}(f_{i})=-(m-i+1)X^{m-i}f_{i-1}\pmod{J_{t}}\quad\textrm{ for all }j=1,\ldots,n,

so for any
j,k\in\left\{1,\ldots n\right\}
we have

X^{m-i}D_{y_{j}-y_{k}}(f_{i})=X^{m-i}D_{y_{j}}(f_{i})-X^{m-i}D_{y_{k}}(f_{i})\in J_{t}

and consequently

X^{m-i}D_{y}(f_{i})\in J_{t}
for all
y\in\mathfrak{h}.

We now consider two cases:

•

t=0
. As
D_{y}(f_{m})=0
for all
y\in\mathfrak{h}

and
M_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right>=L_{0,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)
is irreducible, we conclude that

f_{m}\in\left<v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\right>.

But then

v=f_{m}+X\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}X^{m-i-1}f_{i}\in\left<X,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\right>=J_{0},

which contradicts the assumption that
v\notin J_{0}.

•

t=1
.
As for all
y\in\mathfrak{h}
,
i=0,\ldots,m
we have

X^{m-i}D_{y}(f_{i})\in\left<X^{p}\otimes\tau,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\right>

we can in particular conclude that for all
i
such that
m-i<p
and all
y\in\mathfrak{h}
we have

D_{y}(f_{i})\in\left<v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\right>.

The module
M_{1,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right>=L_{1,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h},\tau)
is irreducible, so

f_{i}\in\left<v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\right>,\quad\textrm{for all }m-p<i\leq m.

But then

v=X^{p}\sum_{i=0}^{m-p}X^{m-p-i}f_{i}+\sum_{i=m-p+1}^{m}X^{m-i}f_{i}\in\left<X^{p}\otimes\tau,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\right>=J_{1},

which contradicts the assumption that
v\notin J_{1}.

∎

We will be working with
H_{t,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h})
but will need to cite several papers which work with
H_{t,c}(S_{n},V)
and we will use Proposition
2.2
to translate between the two.

2.3.
Choices of bases in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}

In order to do explicit computations with Verma modules, we will need to fix bases for
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, all irreducible representations
\tau
of
S_{3}
, and the Verma modules
M_{t,c}(\tau)
. Let
n=3
for this section.

By Section
2.2
, in any characteristic
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
has a basis
\left\{\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{2}}\right\}
, and this is the basis we will use for
p=2,3
.

In characteristic
p>3
, it is convenient to use a basis which is well behaved when restricting from
S_{3}
to
S_{2}=\left\{e,s_{1}\right\}\subseteq S_{3}
, in order to use the additional symmetries to reduce the number of computations (see Lemma
2.20
). We start by identifying

\mathfrak{h}^{*}\cong\left\{\sum_{i}a_{i}x_{i}\mid\sum_{i}a_{i}=0\right\}\subseteq V^{*}

via
\pi
, and then define the
rescaled Young basis
of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
as

\displaystyle b_{+}

\displaystyle=x_{1}+x_{2}-2x_{3}

\displaystyle b_{-}

\displaystyle=x_{1}-x_{2}.

This basis satisfies:

\displaystyle s_{1}.b_{+}=b_{+}\quad s_{1}.b_{-}=-b_{-}\quad b_{-}=\frac{2}{3}\left(s_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)b_{+}.

Conversely, expressing
\pi(\overline{x_{i}})
in this basis we get

\displaystyle\pi(\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle=x_{1}-\frac{X}{3}=\frac{b_{+}+3b_{-}}{6}

\displaystyle\pi(\overline{x_{2}})

\displaystyle=x_{2}-\frac{X}{3}=\frac{b_{+}-3b_{-}}{6}

\displaystyle\pi(\overline{x_{3}})

\displaystyle=x_{3}-\frac{X}{3}=\frac{-b_{+}}{3}.

2.4.
Symmetric polynomials

Singular vectors are in general difficult to describe, but some can always be constructed from symmetric polynomials in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. Specifically, let
(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{3}}_{+}
be the set of symmetric polynomials of strictly positive degree. For all
f\in(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{3}}_{+}
and
v\in\tau
, the vector
f\otimes v
is singular in
M_{0,t}(\tau)
and
f^{p}\otimes v
is singular in
M_{1,t}(\tau)
. Consequently they generate proper submodules, and we can define quotient modules

\displaystyle N_{0,c}(\tau)

\displaystyle=M_{0,c}(\tau)/S\mathfrak{h}^{*}(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{3}}_{+}\otimes\tau,

\displaystyle N_{1,c}(\tau)

\displaystyle=M_{1,c}(\tau)/S\mathfrak{h}^{*}((S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{3}}_{+})^{p}\otimes\tau.

These are called
baby Verma modules
, are finite dimensional, and
L_{t,c}(\tau)
are quotients of
N_{t,c}(\tau)
. We specify their characters for the cases we will need in Lemmas
2.7
and
2.13
.

In order to describe baby Verma modules, but also in order to decompose
M^{k}_{t,c}(\tau)\cong S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\tau
as an
S_{3}
representation (which can help us limit the space in which we are looking for singular vectors), we will need to understand the
S_{3}
invariants in
SV^{*}
and both incarnations of
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. We do this for general
n
because it is just as easy as for
n=3
.

First, by the fundamental theorem of symmetric polynomials, for a field
\Bbbk
of any characteristic,
(SV^{*})^{S_{n}}
is a polynomial algebra in
n
variables of degrees
1,2,\ldots n
, and those variables can be chosen to be the elementary symmetric polynomials

\sigma^{V}_{k}=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq n}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\ldots x_{i_{k}}.

For example, for
n=3
:

\displaystyle\sigma^{V}_{1}

\displaystyle=X=x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}

\displaystyle\sigma^{V}_{2}

\displaystyle=x_{1}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{3}+x_{2}x_{3}

\displaystyle\sigma^{V}_{3}

\displaystyle=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}.

Over some fields there are other good choices of symmetric polynomials as generators of the invariant ring, but this choice works over any field.

The representations
V^{*}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
are faithful representations of
S_{n}
over a field
\Bbbk
of any characteristic
p
unless
(n,p)=(2,2)
, and there is always a surjection
V^{*}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. So, by
[
Nak
]
Prop 4.1, for all
(n,p)\neq(2,2)
,
(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{n}}
is also a polynomial algebra. (Note this is not true for
(S\mathfrak{h})^{S_{n}}
in general.) Let
\overline{\sigma_{i}}
be the image of
\sigma^{V}_{i}
under the quotient map
SV^{*}\to S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. For
n=3
we have:

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma_{2}}

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{3}}+\overline{x_{2}}\overline{x_{3}}=-(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}\overline{x_{3}}=-(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}).

The polynomials
\overline{\sigma_{2}},\overline{\sigma_{3}},\ldots,\overline{\sigma_{n}}
are nonzero, algebraically independent, there are
n-1=\dim\mathfrak{h}^{*}
of them, and the product of their degrees is
\prod_{i=2}^{n}i=n!=|S_{n}|
, so by
[
Kem
]
Prop 16 we can conclude that for all
(n,p)\neq(2,2)

(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{n}}=\Bbbk[\overline{\sigma_{2}},\overline{\sigma_{3}},\ldots,\overline{\sigma_{n}}].

As explained in section
2.2
, in good characteristic (when
p
does not divide
n
),
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
can be alternatively realised as a subrepresentation rather than a quotient of
V^{*}
via the map
\pi
. Call the images of
\overline{\sigma_{i}}
in this realisation

\sigma_{i}=\pi(\overline{\sigma_{i}})\in(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{n}}.

Finally, for
n=3
and
p>3
, let us calculate
\sigma_{i}
explicitly in terms of the basis
b_{-},b_{+}
from Section
2.3
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\pi(\overline{\sigma_{2}})=-\pi(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})=\frac{-1}{12}(b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

(2)

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}

\displaystyle=\pi(\overline{\sigma_{3}})=-\pi(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}^{2})=\frac{-1}{2^{2}3^{3}}(b_{+}^{3}-9b_{+}b_{-}^{2}).

Closely related to the symmetric polynomial are the antisymmetric polynomials,
f\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
which transform as the
\mathrm{Sign}
representation. By considering each transposition in turn and writing out
(ij).f=-f
, one sees that
f
is divisible by
x_{i}-x_{j}
for all
i\neq j
, and consequently that
f
is divisible by the Vandermonde polynomial
q=\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})
. It then quickly follows that any antisymmetric polynomial
f
is of the form
f=q\cdot f^{\prime}
, where
f^{\prime}
is a symmetric polynomial.

For
n=3
we can calculate the Vandermonde polynomial
q
in terms of the basis
b_{-},b_{+}
from Section
2.3
as

q=(x_{1}-x_{2})(x_{2}-x_{3})(x_{1}-x_{3})=b_{+}^{2}b_{-}-b_{-}^{3}.

2.5.
Bases of Verma modules

We will need the following combinatorial lemma.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the number of non-negative integral solutions
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
of the equation
2a+3b=k

equals

\begin{cases}\lfloor\frac{k}{6}\rfloor+1,&k\textrm{ even}\\
\lfloor\frac{k-3}{6}\rfloor+1,&k\textrm{ odd.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We will parametrise the solutions for later use.

First assume
k=2k^{\prime}
is even. The equation
2a+3b=k
then becomes

2a+3b=2k^{\prime}

so we see
b
is even. Write
b=2j
for some
j\geq 0
. The equation now becomes

a=k^{\prime}-3j

which gives another condition
j\leq\frac{k^{\prime}}{3}
.
So, the set of solutions can be parametrised as

\left\{\left(\frac{k}{2}-3j,2j\right)\mid 0\leq j\leq\frac{k}{6}\right\}

and their total number is
\lfloor\frac{k}{6}\rfloor+1
.

Now assume
k=2k^{\prime}+1
is odd. The equation
2a+3b=k
becomes

2a+3b=2k^{\prime}+1

so
b
is odd. Writing
b=2j+1
for some
j\geq 0
we get

a=k^{\prime}-1-3j

which gives another condition
j\leq\frac{k^{\prime}-1}{3}
.
So, the set of solutions can be parametrised as

\left\{\left(\frac{k}{2}-1-3j,2j+1\right)\mid 0\leq j\leq\frac{k-3}{6}\right\}

and their total number is
\lfloor\frac{k-3}{6}\rfloor+1
.

∎

Theorem 2.4
.

In characteristic
p>3
,
S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is a direct sum of the following irreducible
S_{3}
representations:

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k
, a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b+3=k
, a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sign}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}q\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-1
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{+},\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{-}\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-2
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2}),\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}2b_{+}b_{-}\right\}.

Proof.

The
S_{3}
representation
S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is a direct sum of its isotypic components, so it is enough to show that the above vectors are in the correct isotypic components and form a basis.

It is explained at the start of Section
2.4
that the
\mathrm{Triv}
isotypic component is the polynomial algebra in
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
and that the
\mathrm{Triv}
isotypic component consists of products of invariants and the Vandermonde polynomial
q
.

One can check directly that for every
a,b
the span of
\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{+},\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{-}
is an
S_{3}
subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with the isomorphism given by
b_{\pm}\mapsto\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{\pm}
, and that for every choice of
a,b
the span of
\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2}),\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}2b_{+}b_{-}
is an
S_{3}
subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with the isomorphism given by
b_{+}\mapsto\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})
,
b_{-}\mapsto\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}2b_{+}b_{-}.

Next, let us show that the set

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{+},\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{-}\mid 2a+3b+1=k\right\}\cup\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2}),\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}2b_{+}b_{-}\mid 2a+3b+2=k\right\}

is linearly independent. Assume a nontrivial linear combination of these vectors is zero. Gathering the terms, this can be written as

f_{1}\cdot b_{+}+f_{2}\cdot b_{-}+f_{3}\cdot(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+f_{4}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}=0,

where
f_{1},f_{2}
are symmetric polynomials of degree
k-1
,
f_{3},f_{4}
are symmetric polynomials of degree
k-2
, and
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}
are not all zero.
Recall that
b_{+}
and
(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})
are fixed by
s_{1}
, while
b_{-}
and
2b_{+}b_{-}
are multiplied by
-1
by
s_{1}
. If
f_{2},f_{4}
are not both zero, let us apply the projection
\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{id}-s_{1})
to get

f_{2}\cdot b_{-}+f_{4}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}=0.

If
f_{2},f_{4}
are both zero, the expression is

f_{1}\cdot b_{+}+f_{3}\cdot(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})=0,

which after applying
\frac{2}{3}\left(s_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{id}\right)
becomes

f_{1}\cdot b_{-}+f_{3}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}=0.

So, in either case we can assume we have a linear combination of the form

f_{2}\cdot b_{-}+f_{4}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}=0

with
f_{2},f_{4}
symmetric and not both zero. Dividing by
b_{-}
we get

f_{2}=-f_{4}\cdot 2b_{+},

which is an equality between a symmetric polynomial and a polynomial which is not symmetric, so it only holds if
f_{2},f_{4}
are both zero. This is a contradiction, and we conclude that the set

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{+},\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}b_{-}\mid 2a+3b+1=k\right\}\cup\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2}),\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}2b_{+}b_{-}\mid 2a+3b+2=k\right\}

is linearly independent.

We have now seen that the vectors from the statement of the theorem lie in the correct isotypic components and are linearly independent. Their number is equal to

number of vectors

\displaystyle=|\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}\mid 2a+3b=k\right\}|+|\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}\mid 2a+3b+3=k\right\}|+

\displaystyle+2|\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}\mid 2a+3b+1=k\right\}|+2|\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}\mid 2a+3b+2=k\right\}|.

By Lemma
2.3
, if
k
is even, this is equal to

number of vectors

\displaystyle=\lfloor\frac{k}{6}\rfloor+1+\lfloor\frac{k-6}{6}\rfloor+1+2(\lfloor\frac{k-4}{6}\rfloor+1)+2(\lfloor\frac{k-2}{6}\rfloor+1)

\displaystyle=5+2\left(\lfloor\frac{k}{6}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{k-2}{6}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{k-4}{6}\rfloor\right)

\displaystyle=5+2\left(\frac{k}{2}-2\right)=k+1=\dim S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}.

If
k
is odd, then similarly

number of vectors

\displaystyle=\lfloor\frac{k-3}{6}\rfloor+1+\lfloor\frac{k-3}{6}\rfloor+1+2(\lfloor\frac{k-1}{6}\rfloor+1)+2(\lfloor\frac{k-5}{6}\rfloor+1)

\displaystyle=6+2\left(\lfloor\frac{k-1}{6}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{k-3}{6}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{k-5}{6}\rfloor\right)

\displaystyle=6+2\left(\frac{k-1}{2}-2\right)=k+1=\dim S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}.

So, the set of vectors in the statement is a linearly independent set of size equal to the dimension of the vector space, so it is a basis.

∎

We wish to find a nice basis of every Verma module - compatible with the decomposition as an
S_{3}
representation and one in which we can reasonably compute Dunkl operators. For
\tau=\mathrm{Triv}
, we have
M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Triv})\cong S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Triv}\cong S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
as an
S_{3}
representation, so Theorem
2.4
gives us such a basis. A similar computation works when
\tau=\mathrm{Sign}
. For
\tau=\mathrm{Stand}
, we have
M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Stand})\cong S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Stand}
as an
S_{3}
representation, so we need to understand how the above basis of
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
behaves after taking a tensor product with
\mathrm{Stand}
. The following lemma is a standard exercise in representations of finite groups.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
p>3
. As
S_{3}
representations,

(1)

\mathrm{Triv}\otimes\mathrm{Stand}\cong\mathrm{Stand}
tautologically;

(2)

\mathrm{Sign}\otimes\mathrm{Stand}\cong\mathrm{Stand}
with the isomorphism given by

v_{\mathrm{Sign}}\otimes b_{+}\mapsto-3b_{-},\quad v_{\mathrm{Sign}}\otimes b_{-}\mapsto b_{+};

(3)

\mathrm{Stand}\otimes\mathrm{Stand}\cong\mathrm{Triv}\oplus\mathrm{Sign}\oplus\mathrm{Stand}
; with a compatible basis given by

•

b_{+}\otimes b_{+}+3b_{-}\otimes b_{-}
, spanning a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
;

•

b_{+}\otimes b_{-}-b_{-}\otimes b_{+}
spanning a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sign}
;

•

-b_{+}\otimes b_{+}+3b_{-}\otimes b_{-},\,b_{+}\otimes b_{-}+b_{-}\otimes b_{+}
spanning a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
, with the isomorphism given by

b_{+}\mapsto-b_{+}\otimes b_{+}+3b_{-}\otimes b_{-},\quad b_{-}\mapsto b_{+}\otimes b_{-}+b_{-}\otimes b_{+}.

Putting together Theorem
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
we immediately get the following theorem.

Theorem 2.6
.

In characteristic
p>3
,
M^{k}_{t,c}(\mathrm{Stand})\cong S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Stand}
is a direct sum of the following irreducible
S_{3}
representations:

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k
, a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\otimes b_{+},\,\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\otimes b_{-}\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b+3=k
, a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}q\otimes b_{-},\,\frac{-1}{3}\cdot\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}q\otimes b_{+}\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-1
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\cdot(b_{+}\otimes b_{+}+3b_{-}\otimes b_{-})\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-1
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sign}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\cdot(b_{+}\otimes b_{-}-b_{-}\otimes b_{+})\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-1
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\cdot(-b_{+}\otimes b_{+}+3b_{-}\otimes b_{-}),\,\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\cdot(b_{+}\otimes b_{-}+b_{-}\otimes b_{+})\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-2
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\cdot\left((-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})\otimes b_{+}+3\cdot(2b_{+}b_{-})\otimes b_{-}\right)\right\}.

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-2
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sign}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\cdot\left((-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})\otimes b_{-}-(2b_{+}b_{-})\otimes b_{+}\right)\right\}.

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-2
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\left(-(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})\otimes b_{+}+3(2b_{+}b_{-})\otimes b_{-}\right),\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{3}^{b}\cdot\left((-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})\otimes b_{-}+(2b_{+}b_{-})\otimes b_{+}\right)\right\}.

Wherever in this statement a representation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
is given by a basis
\left\{u,v\right\}
, the map
b_{+}\mapsto u,b_{-}\mapsto v
is an
S_{3}
-isomorphism.

Corollary 2.7
.

Assume
p>3
.

(1)

The character of the graded
S_{3}
representation
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is

\chi_{S\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left([\mathrm{Triv}]+(z+z^{2})[\mathrm{Stand}]+z^{3}[\mathrm{Sign}]\right).

(2)

The characters of Verma modules for
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
are given by

\displaystyle\chi_{M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{S\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)

\displaystyle\chi_{M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Sign})}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left([\mathrm{Sign}]+(z+z^{2})[\mathrm{Stand}]+z^{3}[\mathrm{Triv}]\right)

\displaystyle\chi_{M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left((1+z+z^{2}+z^{3})[\mathrm{Stand}]+(z+z^{2})([\mathrm{Triv}]+[\mathrm{Sign}])\right).

(3)

The characters of baby Verma modules for
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
are given by

\displaystyle\chi_{N_{0,c}(\tau)}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{M_{0,c}(\tau)}(z)(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3}).

\displaystyle\chi_{N_{1,c}(\tau)}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{M_{1,c}(\tau)}(z)(1-z^{2p})(1-z^{3p}).

Proof.

By Theorem
2.4

\displaystyle\chi_{S\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b}z^{2a+3b}[\mathrm{Triv}]+\sum_{a,b}z^{2a+3b+3}[\mathrm{Sign}]+\sum_{a,b}(z^{2a+3b+1}+z^{2a+3b+2})[\mathrm{Stand}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left([\mathrm{Triv}]+z^{3}[\mathrm{Sign}]+(z+z^{2})[\mathrm{Stand}]\right).

The characters of Verma and baby Verma modules follow directly.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
S^{(p)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be the quotient of
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
by the ideal generated by
x^{p}
for all
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. For
p>3
, its character is

\displaystyle\chi_{S^{(p)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)=\chi_{S\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)\cdot(1-z^{p}[\mathrm{Stand}]+z^{2p}[\mathrm{Sign}])

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left([\mathrm{Triv}]+z^{3}[\mathrm{Sign}]+(z+z^{2})[\mathrm{Stand}]\right)(1-z^{p}[\mathrm{Stand}]+z^{2p}[\mathrm{Sign}]).

Remark 2.9
.

This is a special case of the formula for an arbitrary representation
U
, stating
\chi_{S^{(p)}U}(z)=\chi_{SU}(z)\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{\dim U}z^{p}[\Lambda^{i}U].

In characteristic
2
the rescaled Young basis
b_{+},b_{-}
does not make sense. (As written,
b_{+}
and
b_{-}
become equal. More generally, this basis relies on restricting
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
to
S_{2}=\left\{e,s_{1}\right\}
and decomposing it into the trivial and sign representation of
S_{2}
, but in characteristic
2
these representations are isomorphic.) Instead, in characteristic
2
we use the basis
\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{2}}
of the standard representation with
\overline{x_{3}}=-\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}=\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}
. The elementary symmetric polynomials in this case are
\overline{\sigma_{2}}
and
\overline{\sigma_{3}}
. The analogue of Theorem
2.4
is the following theorem.

Theorem 2.10
.

In characteristic
p=2
,
S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is a direct sum of the following indecomposable
S_{3}
representations:

•

for every
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a=k
, a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k
,
b>0
, an indecomposable extension of two copies of
\mathrm{Triv}
, with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b},\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-1
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{2}}\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-2
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{1}}^{2},\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right\}.

Proof.

It is clear that for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k
the space spanned by
\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}
is a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
. When
b>0
, it is extended by the one dimensional space with a basis
\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})
, as

\displaystyle s_{1}.(\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3}))

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})+\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}

\displaystyle s_{2}.(\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3}))

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{3}}+\overline{x_{3}}^{3})

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})+\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}.

It is also clear that the space spanned by
\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{2}}\right\}
is a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
via the isomorphism
\overline{x_{i}}\mapsto\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{i}}
and that the space spanned by
\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{1}}^{2},\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right\}
is a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
via the isomorphism
\overline{x_{i}}\mapsto\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{i}}^{2}
.

The set

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b},\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{b-1}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})\mid 2a+3b=k\right\}

is linearly independent, and so is the set

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{1}},\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{2}}\mid 2a+3b=k-1\right\}\cup\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{1}}^{2},\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\mid 2a+3b=k-2\right\}.

The central element
(123)+(132)\in\Bbbk[S_{3}]
acts on the span of the first of these sets by
0
and on the second of them by
1
,
(123)+(132)
and
e-(123)-(132)
act as projections on the span of their union, and this allows us to conclude that the union of these sets is linearly independent as well. To show that it is a basis of
S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, and thus conclude that
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is indeed a direct sum as stated in the theorem, it now suffices to show that the number of elements in this set and compare it to
\dim S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}=k+1
. This is exactly the same calculation as in the proof of Theorem
2.4
.

∎

Next, we will need an analogue of Lemma
2.5
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
p=2
. As an
S_{3}
representation,
\mathrm{Stand}\otimes\mathrm{Stand}
is a direct sum of the following indecomposable subrepresentations:

•

an indecomposable extension of two copies of
\mathrm{Triv}
, with a basis

\left\{\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right\},

with
\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
spanning a subrepresentation of this extension;

•

a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}},\,\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right\},

with the isomorphism given by
\overline{x_{i}}\mapsto\overline{x_{i}}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}
.

Putting together Theorem
2.10
and Lemma
2.11
we get the following theorem.

Theorem 2.12
.

In characteristic
p=2
,
M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Stand})\cong S^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Stand}
is a direct sum of the following indecomposable
S_{3}
representations:

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k
, a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-3
, a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{b}\left((\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right)\right.,

\left.\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{b}\left((\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes(\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})\right)\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-1
,
an indecomposable extension of two copies of
\mathrm{Triv}
, with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})\right\}

of the subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
and a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})\right\}

of the quotient isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
;

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-1
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}),\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})\right\};

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-2
,
an indecomposable extension of two copies of
\mathrm{Triv}
, with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})\right\}

of the subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
and a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})\right\}

of the quotient isomorphic to
\mathrm{Triv}
;

•

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
2a+3b=k-2
,
a subrepresentation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
with a basis

\left\{\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}),\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{a}\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{b}(\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})\right\}.

Corollary 2.13
.

Assume
p=2
.

(1)

The character of the graded
S_{3}
representation
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is

\chi_{S\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left((1+z^{3})[\mathrm{Triv}]+(z+z^{2})[\mathrm{Stand}]\right).

(2)

The character of the graded
S_{3}
representation
S^{(2)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is

\chi_{S^{(2)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)=[\mathrm{Triv}](1+z^{2})+[\mathrm{Stand}]z.

(3)

The characters of Verma modules for
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
are given by

\displaystyle\chi_{M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{S\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)

\displaystyle\chi_{M_{t,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left((1+z+z^{2}+z^{3})[\mathrm{Stand}]+2(z+z^{2})[\mathrm{Triv}]\right).

(4)

The characters of baby Verma modules for
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
are given by

\displaystyle\chi_{N_{0,c}(\tau)}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{M_{0,c}(\tau)}(z)(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3}).

\displaystyle\chi_{N_{1,c}(\tau)}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{M_{1,c}(\tau)}(z)(1-z^{2p})(1-z^{3p}).

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Corollary
2.7
.
∎

2.6.
A few well known lemmas

For later use let us state several well known lemmas and prove some of them. Most of them hold in much greater generality but we only state them for
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
here: Lemma
2.14
,
2.17
and
2.18
extend to all rational Cherednik algebras, Lemma
2.15
to all rational Cherednik algebras and all their characters, and Proposition
2.16
to all rational Cherednik algebras of type
A
.

Lemma 2.14
.

For every
a\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, the map
\Psi:H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})\to H_{at,ac}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
given on the generators
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*},y\in\mathfrak{h},g\in S_{3}

\Psi(x)=ax,\quad\Psi(y)=y,\quad\Psi(g)=g

extends uniquely to an isomorphism of algebras.

As a consequence, it is enough to consider just
t=0
and
t=1
.

Lemma 2.15
.

(1)

The map
\Phi:H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})\to H_{t,-c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
given on the generators
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*},y\in\mathfrak{h},s_{1},s_{2}\in S_{3}
by

\Phi(x)=x,\quad\Phi(y)=y,\quad\Phi(s_{i})=-s_{i}

is an isomorphism of algebras.

(2)

The pullback map
\Phi^{*}:\mathcal{O}_{t,-c}\to\mathcal{O}_{t,c}
is an equivalence of categories and

\Phi^{*}(L_{t,-c}(\tau))\cong L_{t,c}(\mathrm{Sign}\otimes\tau).

Recall that the behaviour of Cherednik algebras depends on the parameter
c
in a specific way: for generic
c
the Calogero-Moser space is smooth and irreducible representations are “big” (in characteristic
0
this specifically means equal to Verma modules), while for special
c
the Calogero-Moser space is singular and irreducible representations are smaller than for generic
c
(in terms of dimension or Hilbert series).

Proposition 2.16

(
[
Lia12
]
Prop 2.8)
.

For the rational Cherednik algebra
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
:

(1)

If
t=0
, all
c\neq 0
are generic.

(2)

If
t=1
, all
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{p}
are generic.

Proof.

For
t=0
see Lemma
2.14
, for
t=1
see
[
Lia12
]
Proposition 2.8.
∎

Lemma 2.17
.

The irreducible representation
L_{0,0}(\tau)
is the quotient of the Verma module
M_{0,0}(\tau)
by all the positively graded vectors, with the character

\chi_{L_{0,0}(\tau)}(z)=[\tau]

and the
Hilbert polynomial

h_{L_{0,0}(\tau)}(z)=\dim\tau.

Proof.

When
t=c=0
, the Dunkl operators are all identically equal to
0
, so all vectors of strictly positive degree are singular. Thus,
L_{0,0}(\tau)
is concentrated in degree
0
, where it equals
\tau
.
∎

Lemma 2.18
.

The irreducible representation
L_{1,0}(\tau)
is the quotient of the Verma module
M_{1,0}(\tau)
by all the vectors of the form
x^{p}\otimes v
for any
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
v\in\tau
.
It has the character

\chi_{L_{1,0}(\tau))}(z)=\chi_{S^{(p)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)\cdot[\tau]

and the
Hilbert polynomial

h_{L_{1,0}(\tau)}(z)=\left(\frac{1-z^{p}}{1-z}\right)^{\dim\mathfrak{h}}\dim\tau.

Proof.

This is well known, see also
[
Lia12
]
. When
t=1
and
c=0
, the Dunkl operators have a very simple form
D_{y}=\partial_{y}\otimes\mathrm{id},

and their joint kernel consists of vectors of the form
x^{p}\otimes v
for all
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
v\in\tau
.
∎

We now rephrase Theorem 3.2 from
[
Lia12
]
in our conventions (using
\mathfrak{h}
instead of
V
) and state a slight strengthening of it with the same proof.

Lemma 2.19

(
[
Lia12
]
Theorem 3.2)
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p
. The character of the irreducible representation
L_{t,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
of
H_{t,c}(S_{n})
is equal to
[\mathrm{Triv}]
if and only if
t=cn
. In particular, this holds when
t=c=0
, and when
p
does not divide
n
and the values of the parameters are
t=1,c=1/n
.

Proof.

It suffices to note that

\displaystyle D_{y_{i}-y_{j}}(\overline{x_{k}})

\displaystyle=t\cdot(\delta_{ij}-\delta_{jk})-c\left(\sum_{a<b}\left<y_{i}-y_{j},\overline{x_{a}}-\overline{x_{b}}\right>\frac{\overline{x_{k}}-(ab)\overline{x_{k}}}{\overline{x_{a}}-\overline{x_{b}}}\right)

\displaystyle=t\cdot(\delta_{ij}-\delta_{jk})-c\left(\sum_{a}\left<y_{i}-y_{j},\overline{x_{k}}-\overline{x_{a}}\right>\right)

\displaystyle=(t-cn)\cdot(\delta_{ij}-\delta_{jk}).

This shows that all
\overline{x_{k}}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}\cong M_{t,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Triv})
are singular if and only if
t=cn
.

∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Assume that
p>3
,
M
is a Verma module
M_{t,c}(\tau)
or its quotient, and that a homogeneous
v\in M
is either
S_{3}
invariant or anti-invariant. Then
v
is singular if and only if
D_{y_{1}}v=0.

Proof.

First, extend the action of
y\in\mathfrak{h}
to the action of all
y\in V
by the same formula for Dunkl operators. This amounts to letting
Y=y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}
act by
0
.

\Rightarrow

Assume
v
satisfies
D_{y_{i}-y_{j}}(v)=0
for all
i,j
. Then

D_{y_{1}}(v)=\frac{1}{3}\left(D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}(v)+D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(v)+D_{Y}(v)\right)=0

as claimed. (Notice that this argument fails in characteristic
3
.)

\Leftarrow

Assume
D_{y_{1}}(v)=0
and
s.v=\epsilon v
for all
s\in S
and
\epsilon=\pm 1
. Then

\displaystyle D_{y_{2}}(v)

\displaystyle=(12)D_{y_{1}}((12).v)=\epsilon(12)D_{y_{1}}(v)=0,

\displaystyle D_{y_{3}}(v)

\displaystyle=D_{Y}(v)-D_{y_{1}}(v)-D_{y_{2}}(v)=0,

so
D_{y}(v)=0
for all
y\in V
and in particular all
y\in\mathfrak{h}.

∎

.
