3.1.
The irreducible representation
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
in characteristic
2
for
c\neq 0

We start by citing a theorem from
[
CK
]
.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
CK
]
Theorem 2.11.)
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
2
, let
n
be an arbitrary odd integer,
t=0
and
c\in\Bbbk
be generic. The singular vectors in
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
which generate the maximal proper graded submodule are
\overline{x_{i}}^{2}+\overline{x_{i}}\overline{x_{j}}+\overline{x_{j}}^{2},\overline{x_{i}}\overline{x_{j}}\overline{x_{k}},\quad i<j<k<n.
The Hilbert polynomial of the irreducible representation
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
of
H_{0,c}(S_{n})
equals

h_{L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)=(1+z)(1+(n-2)z+z^{2}).

We reprove a special case of this theorem, while also calculating the character of the representation
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
and clarifying that “generic
c
” in this case means
c\neq 0
. The special case we will prove is the following lemma. The proof is following the ideas of
[
CK
]
and simplifying where possible.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
2
, and let the values of the parameters be
t=0
and
c\neq 0
. The irreducible representation
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
of
H_{0,c}(S_{3})
is equal to the baby Verma module
N_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
, with the character

\chi_{L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)=[\mathrm{Triv}]+[\mathrm{Stand}]z+[\mathrm{Stand}]z^{2}+[\mathrm{Triv}]z^{3}

and the
Hilbert polynomial

h_{L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)=(1+z)(1+z+z^{2})=1+2z+2z^{2}+z^{3}.

Proof.

When
n=3
, the elementary symmetric polynomials
\overline{\sigma_{2}}
and
\overline{\sigma_{3}}
in
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
are exactly the vectors from Theorem
3.2
. The quotient of
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
by the submodule generated by these vectors is the baby Verma module
N_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
, which has the above stated character and Hilbert series by Corollary
2.13
.

It remains to show that when
c\neq 0
, the baby Verma module
N_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
is irreducible. We do this by checking that there are no singular vectors in it.

For any
a_{1},a_{2}\in\Bbbk
and
i=1,2
we directly calculate that

\displaystyle D_{y_{i}-y_{3}}(a_{1}\overline{x_{1}}+a_{2}\overline{x_{2}})

\displaystyle=ca_{i}.

As
c\neq 0
this shows there are no singular vectors in
N_{0,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Triv})
.

A basis of
M_{0,c}^{2}(\mathrm{Triv})\cong S^{2}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is
\left\{\overline{x_{1}}^{2},\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}},\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right\}
, and
\overline{\sigma_{2}}
is their sum. After taking the quotient by
\overline{\sigma_{2}}
, we choose
\left\{\overline{x_{1}}^{2},\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right\}
as a basis of
N_{0,c}^{2}(\mathrm{Triv})
. To look for singular vectors in
N_{0,c}^{2}(\mathrm{Triv})
we calculate, for
a_{1},a_{2}\in\Bbbk

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(a_{1}\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+a_{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=-c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(12)}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}(a_{1}\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+a_{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2})-c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(23)}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}(a_{1}\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+a_{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=c\frac{(a_{1}+a_{2})(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}-\overline{x_{2}}^{2})}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}+c\frac{a_{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}-a_{2}\overline{x_{3}}^{2}}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}

\displaystyle=c\left(a_{1}\overline{x_{1}}+(a_{1}+a_{2})\overline{x_{2}}\right)\neq 0.

This shows that there are no singular vectors in
N_{0,c}^{2}(\mathrm{Triv})
.

Finally, a basis of
N_{0,c}^{3}(\mathrm{Triv})
is
\left\{\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}\right\}
, and we have

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}})

\displaystyle=-c\frac{\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}-c\frac{\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{3}}}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}=-c\left(\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\right),

which is not zero in
N_{0,c}^{2}(\mathrm{Triv})
, showing there are no singular vectors in
N_{0,c}^{3}(\mathrm{Triv})
.
∎

3.2.
The irreducible representation
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
in characteristic
2
for generic
c

Again, we start by citing a theorem from
[
CK
]
.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
CK
]
Theorem 3.17.)
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field , let
n
be an arbitrary odd integer,
t=1
and
c\in\Bbbk
be transcendental over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. The Hilbert polynomial of the irreducible representation
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
of
H_{1,c}(S_{n},\mathfrak{h})
equals

h_{L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)=(1+z)^{n-1}(1+(n-1)z^{2}+(n-1)z^{4}+z^{6}).

Note that the reduced Hilbert polynomial (see
[
BCb
]
Proposition 3.4]) of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
equals the Hilbert polynomial of
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
.

Let us reprove a special case
n=3
of this theorem, while also calculating the character of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
and clarifying that generic
c
means
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
2
,
t=1
and
c\neq 0,1
. The irreducible representation
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
equals the baby Verma module
N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
with

\displaystyle\chi_{L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)

\displaystyle=([\mathrm{Triv}]+[\mathrm{Stand}]z+[\mathrm{Triv}]z^{2})([\mathrm{Triv}]+[\mathrm{Stand}]z^{2}+[\mathrm{Stand}]z^{4}+[\mathrm{Triv}]z^{6})

\displaystyle h_{L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)

\displaystyle=(1+z)^{2}(1+2z^{2}+2z^{4}+z^{6}).

Proof.

The only thing to check is that for
c\neq 0,1
the baby Verma module
N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
is irreducible, as its character has been computed in Corollary
2.13
.

Assume that
U\neq 0
is a submodule of
N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
. The module
N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
is
a Frobenius algebra, in the sense that for every
k
the multiplication
N_{1,c}^{k}(\mathrm{Triv})\times N_{1,c}^{8-k}(\mathrm{Triv})\to N_{1,c}^{8}(\mathrm{Triv})
is a nondegenerate pairing. Using the action of
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}\subseteq H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
, this implies
\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\neq 0\in U
. Using the action of
S\mathfrak{h}\subseteq H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
, we get that

D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{3}D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{5}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})\in U.

Let us calculate this vector. We work with the basis
\left\{\overline{x_{1}}^{i}\overline{x_{2}}^{j}\mid i<6,j<4\right\}
of
N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
, and use that
\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{3}}=0
,
\overline{\sigma_{2}^{2}}=0
, and
\overline{\sigma_{3}^{2}}=0
in
N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
.

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=5\cdot\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-c\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-\overline{x_{1}}^{3}\overline{x_{2}}^{5}}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}+\frac{\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{3}}^{3}}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}\right)

\displaystyle=(1+c)\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{2}}^{3},

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{2}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(1+c)\left(4\cdot\overline{x_{1}}^{3}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-c\left(\frac{\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-\overline{x_{1}}^{3}\overline{x_{2}}^{4}}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}+\frac{\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{3}}^{3}}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(1+c)c\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}^{3}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right)

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{3}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(1+c)c\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right),

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{4}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(1+c)c^{2}\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right)

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{5}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(1+c)c^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{3},

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{5}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(1+c)c^{2}\left(-3\overline{x_{2}}^{2}-c\left(\frac{\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-\overline{x_{2}}^{3}}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{3}}}-\frac{\overline{x_{2}}^{3}-\overline{x_{3}}^{3}}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(1+c)c^{2}\left(c\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+c\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+(1+c)\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right),

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{2}D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{5}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})=

\displaystyle=(1+c)c^{3}\overline{x_{2}},

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{3}D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}^{5}(\overline{x_{1}}^{5}\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(1+c)^{2}c^{3}.

So,
(1+c)^{2}c^{3}\in U
, and for
c\neq 0,1
this implies
1\in U
. Using
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
action this implies
U=N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
and shows that
N_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
is irreducible.
∎

3.3.
The irreducible representation
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
in characteristic
p\neq 3
for generic
c

In the following sections we aim to describe
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
2
, but the proofs work for any characteristic
p\neq 3
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
p,t
and
c
the matrices of the Dunkl operators
D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}
and
D_{y_{2}-y_{3}}
on
M_{t,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})
written in the bases
\left\{\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}},\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right\}
for
M_{t,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})
and
\left\{1\otimes\overline{x_{1}},1\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right\}
for
M_{t,c}^{0}(\mathrm{Stand})
are:

\left[(D_{y_{1}-y_{2}})|_{M_{t,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})}\right]=\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}}&{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}}&{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}}&{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}}\\
t+c&-2c&-t+c&c&{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\overline{x_{1}}}\\
-c&t-c&2c&-t-c&{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\overline{x_{2}}}\\
\end{array}

\left[(D_{y_{2}-y_{3}})|_{M_{t,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})}\right]=\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}}&{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}}&{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}}&{\scriptstyle\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}}\\
c&c&t-2c&c&{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\overline{x_{1}}}\\
2c&-c&-c&t+2c&{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\overline{x_{2}}}\\
\end{array}

Proof.

Direct computation.

∎

Lemma 3.7
.

(1)

For any
p
the following vectors in
M_{0,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are singular:

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=-\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle=2\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}.

(2)

For
p\neq 3
and
c\neq 0
,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
span the set of singular vectors in
M_{0,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})
.

Proof.

As
\left\{y_{1}-y_{2},y_{2}-y_{3}\right\}
span
\mathfrak{h}
, the intersection of the kernels of
D_{y}
for all
y\in\mathfrak{h}
equals
\ker(y_{1}-y_{2})\cap\ker(y_{2}-y_{3})
.

(1)

This follows immediately from Lemma
3.6
by setting
t=0
.

(2)

The determinant of the leftmost
2\times 2
minor of
(D_{y_{1}-y_{2}})|_{M_{t,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})}
equals

(t+c)(t-c)-(-c)(-2c)=t^{2}-3c^{2}
, which is nonzero when
p\neq 3
,
t=0
and
c\neq 0
. This shows that
(D_{y_{1}-y_{2}})|_{M_{t,c}^{1}(\mathrm{Stand})}
has rank
2
so its kernel has dimension
2
.

∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
p\neq 3
,
t=0
,
c\neq 0
, and
v_{1},v_{2}\in M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
from Lemma
3.7
.

(1)

The subrepresentation
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
is isomorphic to
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})[-1]
.

(2)

The Hilbert series of the quotient
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
is
\frac{2(1-z)}{(1-z)^{2}}
.

(3)

For
i=1,2
the vectors
\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}
lie in
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
.

(4)

For
i=1,2
the vectors
\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}
,
i=1,2
do not lie in
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
.

(5)

The quotient
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>

has the character

\chi_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}]+([\mathrm{Triv}]+[\mathrm{Sign}])z+[\mathrm{Stand}]z^{2}

and the Hilbert polynomial

h_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{j}}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},j=2,3,i=1,2\right>}(z)=2+2z+2z^{2}.

(6)

The quotient
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
is irreducible.

Proof.

(1)

The vectors
v_{1},v_{2}
are singular and span an
S_{3}
-subrepresentation isomorphic to the standard representation via the isomorphism

\varphi:\mathrm{Stand}\to\mathrm{span}\left\{v_{1},v_{2}\right\}

given by
\varphi(\overline{x_{i}})=v_{i}.

By the universal mapping property
\varphi
extends to a homomorphism of graded
H_{0,c}(S_{3})
representations

\varphi:M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})[-1]\to M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand}),

given, for
f\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
i=1,2
, by

\varphi(f\otimes\overline{x_{i}})=f\cdot v_{i}.

We claim
\varphi
is injective.

Assume that some
v\in M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
is in the kernel of
\varphi
. Without loss of generality
v
is homogeneous, so there are
A,B\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
homogeneous of the same degree such that

v=A\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+B\otimes\overline{x_{2}}.

Applying
\varphi
we get

Av_{1}+Bv_{2}=\varphi(v)=0,

which leads to

\displaystyle(-\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})A+(2\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})B

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}})A+(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})B

\displaystyle=0.

Considering this as a system of equations with unknowns
A,B\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, we calculate its determinant as

\displaystyle\det

\displaystyle=(-\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})-(2\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})(\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}})

\displaystyle=-3(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})=-3\overline{\sigma_{2}}.

Whenever
p\neq 3
, this is nonzero and the above system has only the trivial solution
A=B=0
, so the kernel of the
H_{0,c}(S_{3})
homomorphism
\varphi
contains only
v=0
, and
\varphi
is injective.

(2)

It immediately follows that

h_{\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>}(z)=z\cdot h_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=\frac{2z}{(1-z)^{2}}

and

h_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>}(z)=\frac{2(1-z)}{(1-z)^{2}}.

(3)

Checking whether a vector
u=u_{1}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+u_{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}
is in the subrepresentation generated by
v_{1},v_{2}
is equivalent to solving the system

Av_{1}+Bv_{2}=u,

for
A,B\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
.
Reading off the coefficients of
\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
and
\otimes\overline{x_{2}}
we get this is equivalent to the system of equations

\displaystyle(-\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})A+(2\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})B

\displaystyle=u_{1}

\displaystyle(\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}})A+(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})B

\displaystyle=u_{2}.

This system has a determinant
\det=-3\overline{\sigma_{2}}
. When
p\neq 3
, its unique solutions
A,B
, as rational functions on
\mathfrak{h}
, as given as:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{1}(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})+u_{2}(2\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})}{-3\overline{\sigma_{2}}}

(3)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{1}(\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}})+u_{2}(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})}{3\overline{\sigma_{2}}}.

The question on whether
u=u_{1}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+u_{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}
is in the subrepresentation generated by
v_{1},v_{2}
is equivalent to checking whether these
A,B
are polynomials in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
(as opposed to rational functions).

When
u=\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
we have
u_{1}=\overline{\sigma_{2}}
,
u_{2}=0
so the solutions given by equations (
3
) are indeed in
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, and simplify as

A=\frac{-1}{3}(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}),\quad B=\frac{1}{3}(\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}}).

This shows
\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}\in\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
, and
\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\in\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
follows by the action of
S_{3}
.

(4)

Analogously, to check whether
\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
,
i=1
is in
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
, we substitute
u_{1}=\overline{\sigma_{3}},u_{2}=0
into (
3
), getting

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})}{-3\overline{\sigma_{2}}}=\frac{\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})}{3(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})}

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}})}{3\overline{\sigma_{2}}}=\frac{\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})(\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}})}{-3(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})}.

In characteristics other than
p=3
the numerators and denominators of these rational functions are coprime, so
A,B
are not polynomials. We conclude that
\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
is not in the subrepresentation generated by
v_{1},v_{2}
. Similar argument, or the action of
S_{3}
, gives that
\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}
is not in
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
.

(5)

Using (1), (3) and (4), and letting
O(z^{4})
denotes some series with terms of degree greater or equal to
4
, the first terms of this character are:

\displaystyle\chi_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{j}}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},j=2,3,i=1,2\right>}(z)=

\displaystyle\quad=\chi_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-\chi_{\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>}(z)-\chi_{\left<\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\sigma_{3}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}\right>}(z)+O(z^{4})

\displaystyle\quad=\chi_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-z\cdot\chi_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-z^{3}\cdot\chi_{M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)+O(z^{4})

\displaystyle\quad=[\mathrm{Stand}]+([\mathrm{Triv}]+[\mathrm{Sign}])z+[\mathrm{Stand}]z^{2}+O(z^{4}).

This shows the quotient is concentrated in degrees
0,1,2
, and its character and Hilbert polynomial are as claimed.

(6)

For
p>3
, we could use
[
DSa
]
Prop 4.1. to deduce that
L_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
and
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
have the same Hilbert polynomial, and thus are equal. When
p=2
the results of
[
DSa
]
do not apply to
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
. Instead, we will prove that
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
is irreducible by a direct computation which works for all
p\neq 3
.

If it is not irreducible, then it has singular vectors. These cannot be in degree
0
(by definition) or degree
1
by Lemma
3.7
(2), so we just examine degree
2
.

Degree
2
of
M_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
has a basis

\left\{\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}},\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}},\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right\},

so
M^{2}_{0,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
has a basis

\left\{\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right\}.

Calculate the action of a Dunkl operator
D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}
in this basis as

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle=-c\left(2\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+3\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})

\displaystyle=-c\left(2\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+2\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right).

For
c\neq 0
these are linearly independent so no nontrivial linear combination of
D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})
and
D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})
is zero.
This proves the statement.

∎

3.4.
The irreducible representation
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
in characteristic
2
for generic
c

For this subsection, let
p=2
,
t=1
, and
c
be generic. We will describe
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
, give the generators of the maximal proper graded submodule
J_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
of the Verma modules
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
, calculate the character and Hilbert polynomial of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
, and clarify again that
c
generic here means
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

The strategy is similar to the strategy we use later for
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
in characteristic
p>3
, but we need to treat the cases separately, because we cannot use the rescaled Young basis
b_{+},b_{-}
of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
in characteristic
2
(see Section
2.3
).

The action of the Casimir element
\Omega
tells us where to look for singular vectors in this case, by the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
p=2
,
M
be an
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
module equal to the Verma module
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
or its quotient, and
\tau\subseteq M^{k}
an irreducible
S_{3}
-subrepresentation contained in the kernel of all Dunkl operators.

(1)

The action of the Casimir element
\Omega
on
\tau
is by a scalar:

\Omega|_{\mathrm{Triv}}=0\cdot\mathrm{id},\quad\Omega|_{\mathrm{Stand}}=c\cdot\mathrm{id}.

(2)

If
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{2}
then
\tau=\mathrm{Stand}
and
k
is even.

(3)

If
c=1
then either
\tau=\mathrm{Stand}
and
k
is even, or
\tau=\mathrm{Triv}
and
k
is odd.

Proof.

(1)

This is Lemma
2.1
for
p=2
.

(2)

The action of
\Omega
on
M_{1,c}^{k}(\mathrm{Stand})
and any of its quotients is by a scalar
c+k
. Simultaneously part (1) gives the constant for the action on
\tau\subseteq M^{k}
. If
\tau=\mathrm{Triv}
, comparing these constants leads to

c+k=0

which has no solutions for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
c\notin\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
If
\tau=\mathrm{Stand}
, this leads to

c+k=c

which implies
k
is even.

(3)

Similarly, for
\tau=\mathrm{Triv}
we get an equation
c+k=0
which is satisfied when
c=1
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
is odd, and for
\tau=\mathrm{Stand}
we get an equation
c+k=c
which is satisfied when
c=1
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
is even.

∎

We now look for singular vectors for generic
c
, knowing they will be in the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic components of
M_{1,c}^{2k^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Stand})
. The first ones we find are in degree
2
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For every
c
the vectors

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=c\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle=c\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+(\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

in
M_{1,c}^{2}(\mathrm{Stand})
span an
S_{3}
representation isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stand}
and are singular.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.12
the map
\overline{x_{i}}\mapsto v_{i}
is an
S_{3}
isomorphism, so
v_{1},v_{2}
span a copy of
\mathrm{Stand}
. To see it consists of singular vectors, we first directly calculate

D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(v_{1}+v_{2})=0,

and from here

D_{y_{2}-y_{3}}(v_{1}+v_{2})=s_{1}D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}s_{1}(v_{1}+v_{2})=s_{1}D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(v_{1}+v_{2})=s_{1}.0=0,

showing
v_{1}+v_{2}
is singular. Then
v_{2}=s_{2}.(v_{1}+v_{2})
and
v_{1}=s_{1}.v_{2}
are singular too.
∎

We will now need to investigate what the
H_{1,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
subrepresentation
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
looks like. Checking whether some vector
u=u_{1}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+u_{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\in M^{k}_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
is in
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
amounts to solving the system

Av_{1}+Bv_{2}=u,

for
A,B\in S^{k-2}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
.
Reading off the coefficients of
\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
and
\otimes\overline{x_{2}}
we get

\displaystyle((c+1)\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+c\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+(c+1)\overline{x_{2}}^{2})A+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}B

\displaystyle=u_{1}

(4)

\displaystyle\overline{x_{1}}^{2}A+((c+1)\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+c\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+(c+1)\overline{x_{2}}^{2})B

\displaystyle=u_{2}.

Lemma 3.11
.

For
p=2
, the determinant of the system (
4
) is

\det=(c+1)^{2}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}.

When
c\neq 1
, the unique rational functions
A,B
on
\mathfrak{h}
solving that system are:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{1}((c+1)\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+c\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+(c+1)\overline{x_{2}}^{2})-u_{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}}{(c+1)^{2}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}}

(5)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{-u_{1}\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+u_{2}((c+1)\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+c\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+(c+1)\overline{x_{2}}^{2})}{(c+1)^{2}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}}.

Proof.

We calculate directly:

\displaystyle\det

\displaystyle=((c+1)\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+c\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+(c+1)\overline{x_{2}}^{2})^{2}-\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(c+1)^{2}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})^{2}=(c+1)^{2}\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}.

∎

We proceed in a way analogous to Lemma
3.8
.

Lemma 3.12
.

When
p=2
,
t=1
and
c\neq 1
, the vectors
v_{1},v_{2}\in M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
from Lemma
3.10

generate a subrepresentation of
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
isomorphic to
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})[-2]
. The Hilbert series of the quotient
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
is
\frac{2(1-z^{2})}{(1-z)^{2}}
.

Proof.

The isomorphism of
S_{3}
representations

\varphi:\mathrm{Stand}\to\mathrm{span}\left\{v_{1},v_{2}\right\}

given by
\varphi(\overline{x_{i}})=v_{i}
extends to a homomorphism of
H_{1,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
modules

\varphi:M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})[-2]\to M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand}).

Assuming some nonzero vector
A\overline{x_{1}}+B\overline{x_{2}}
is in the kernel of
\varphi
is equivalent to assuming there is a nontrivial solution
A,B\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
to the equation

Av_{1}+Bv_{2}=0.

This is equivalent to there existing a nontrivial solution to the system (
4
) with
u_{1}=u_{2}=0
, which is impossible by Lemma
3.11
.
∎

Next, we consider
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
and check whether the
p
-th powers of the invariants give us a proper submodule of it.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c\neq 1
. For
i=1,2
we have

\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}\in\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>,\qquad\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}\notin\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>.

Proof.

A vector
\overline{\sigma_{j}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}
,
i=1,2
,
j=2,3
is in the submodule generated by
v_{1},v_{2}
if and only if the system (
4
) with
u_{i}=\overline{\sigma_{j}}^{2}
,
u_{k}=0
for
k\neq i
has polynomial solutions
A,B\in S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. Lemma
3.11
gives these solutions explicitly as rational functions. If
j=2
, these rational functions are in fact polynomial, as the factors
\overline{\sigma_{j}}^{2}
in the numerator and the denominator cancel. If
j=3
, these rational functions are not polynomial, as the numerator and the denominator are coprime.
∎

Next we consider
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
. First we use Lemma
3.13
to calculate its character, and then we will show it is irreducible.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c\neq 1
.
The module
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{i},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},i=1,2\right>
has the character

\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{i},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},i=1,2\right>}(z)=\chi_{S^{(2)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)\left([\mathrm{Stand}](1+z^{4})+2[\mathrm{Triv}]z^{2}\right)

and the Hilbert polynomial

h_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{i},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},i=1,2\right>}(z)=2\frac{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{6})}{(1-z)^{2}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.13
, the quotient
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
has the character

\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)\cdot(1-z^{2}).

Using Lemma
3.13

\displaystyle\chi

{}_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>}(z)=

\displaystyle=\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-\chi_{\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>}(z)-\chi_{\left<\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>}(z)+\chi_{\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>\cap\left<\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-z^{2}\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-z^{6}\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)+\chi_{\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>\cap\left<\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>}(z).

The remaining task is to describe the submodule
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>\cap\left<\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
in enough detail. We first claim that it contains no vectors in degree
7
.

This intersection is a submodule of
\left<\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
, which is isomorphic to a quotient of
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})[-6]
. If the intersection was nonzero in graded degree
7
, then
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})[-6]
would have a nontrivial submodule starting in degree
7
, so
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
would have a nontrivial submodule starting in degree
1
. This means there would be a singular vector in degree
1
of
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
, which is impossible by Lemma
3.9
.

Thus,

\displaystyle\chi

{}_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{j}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},j=2,3,i=1,2\right>}(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-z^{2}\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-z^{6}\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)+O(z^{8}),

where
O(z^{8})
denotes a polynomial with terms of degrees
8
and more.
Calculating the coefficient of
z^{7}
in that expression, we get it is equal to
0
. This means the module
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{j}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},j=2,3,i=1,2\right>
is concentrated in degrees
0,\ldots,6
, and its character is equal to
the truncation of
(1-z^{2}-z^{6})\chi_{M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)
at
z^{7}
, which can be easily calculated to be
as claimed. The Hilbert polynomial follows.

∎

It remains to see that
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
is irreducible, which we do by showing it has no singular vectors. By Lemma
3.9
, we only need to examine its even degrees, and only look for singular vectors in the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic components.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c\neq 1
.
The module

M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>

has no singular vectors of type
\mathrm{Stand}
in degree
2
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.12
, the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component of
M^{2}_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
has a basis:

\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}},

\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\,\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}.

Let us consider just the part of these copies of
\mathrm{Stand}
corresponding to
\overline{x_{1}}
(this is enough as
\mathrm{Stand}
is irreducible), so the part spanned by

\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\,\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}.

These vectors satisfy

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle=c\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}.

Their only linear combination which is singular is

v_{1}=c\cdot\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}),

so there are no singular vectors in
M_{1,c}^{2}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{i},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}},i=1,2\right>
.

∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c\neq 1
.
The module

M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>

has no singular vectors of type
\mathrm{Stand}
in degree
4
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.12
, the part of the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component of
M^{4}_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
corresponding to
\overline{x_{1}}
has a basis

\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\,\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}),\,\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}).

Using Theorem
2.12
again, we see that the submodule
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
which we quotient by contains the following vectors in the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component of degree
4
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}v_{1}

\displaystyle=c\cdot\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle\overline{x_{1}}^{2}v_{1}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}v_{1}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}v_{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+c\cdot\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}).

As
c\neq 1
, these two vectors are linearly independent, and so
\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
and
\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})
lie in
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
, while
\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})
does not and spans the
\overline{x_{1}}
part of the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component of
M_{1,c}^{4}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{j}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}\right>
.

It remains to check if
\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})
is singular in the quotient
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>.
We calculate

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}))

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}},

which can be shown either directly or using Lemma
3.11
to not lie in
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
, so is not zero in the quotient
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
.

∎

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c\neq 1
.
The module

M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>

has no singular vectors of type
\mathrm{Stand}
in degree
6
.

Proof.

Using Theorem
2.12
, the part of the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component of
M^{6}_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
corresponding to
\overline{x_{1}}
has a basis

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{3}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})\otimes\overline{x_{1}}.

Taking a quotient by
\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}
annihilates
\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}=D,
taking a quotient by
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
annihilates (again using Theorem
2.12
)

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}v_{1}

\displaystyle=B+c\cdot C

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}v_{1}+\overline{x_{2}}v_{1}+\overline{x_{1}}v_{2})

\displaystyle=c\cdot A+E

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}v_{1}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}v_{1}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}v_{2})

\displaystyle=c\cdot B+C.

and the resulting basis for the
\overline{x_{1}}
part of the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component in the quotient
M^{6}_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{j}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{i}}\right>
is
A
. To check that it is not singular in this quotient, we calculate

D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(A)=\overline{x_{1}}^{4}\overline{x_{2}}\otimes(\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}),

which can be shown directly or using Lemma
3.11
does not lie in the submodule
\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>
.

∎

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c\neq 0,1
.
The quotient of the Verma module
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
by the submodule generated by
v_{1},v_{2}
from Lemma
3.10
,
\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
and
\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}
is irreducible, and thus equal to
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
,
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{2},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}\right>
has no singular vectors in odd degrees, and only singular vectors of type
\mathrm{Stand}
in even degrees. By Lemma
3.14
, the only positive even degrees of this module are
2,4
and
6
, in which there are no singular vectors of type
\mathrm{Stand}
by Lemmas
3.15
,
3.16
and
3.17
. Hence, this module has no singular vectors and is irreducible.

∎

3.5.
The irreducible representation
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
in characteristic
2
for
c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}

The previous section worked out the characters of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Stand})
for all
c\neq 0,1
. For
c=0
, this character is computed in Lemma
2.18
. In this section we compute it for
c=1
. For the entire section, let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c=1
. The aim of the section is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
p=2
,
t=1
,
c=1
. The irreducible representation
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
is the quotient of the Verma module
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
by the submodule generated by the following vectors:

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}

\displaystyle v_{3}

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})

\displaystyle v_{5}

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle v_{7}

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}).

The character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
is

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+z^{2}+2z^{3}+z^{4}+z^{5}+z^{6})+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+2z^{2}+2z^{4}+z^{5})

and its Hilbert polynomial is

h_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=2+3z+4z^{2}+4z^{3}+4z^{4}+3z^{5}+2z^{6}=\frac{1-z-z^{3}-z^{5}-z^{7}+2z^{8}}{(1-z)^{2}}.

We prove this in the series of lemmas, considering one degree of
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
at a time, and calculating the character one term at a time.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
p=2
. The space of singular vectors in
M^{1}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
is spanned by

v_{1}=\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}.

Hence, the first terms of the character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+\ldots)+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+\ldots).

Proof.

Follows directly from Lemma
3.6
, by substituting
t=c=1
into the matrices of Dunkl operators calculated there and looking for the intersection of their kernels.
∎

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
p=2
. There are no singular vectors in
M^{2}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1}\right>
.
The first terms of the character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+z^{2}\ldots)+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+2z^{2}\ldots).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
we only need to inspect the
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component for singular vectors. The part of it corresponding to
\overline{x_{1}}
in
M^{2}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
has a basis

\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}},\quad\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}.

Taking the quotient by
\left<v_{1}\right>
annihilates the vector

x_{1}v_{1}=(\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})+(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}).

So, it is enough to check that
\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}
is not singular. We calculate

D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})=\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}\notin\left<v_{1}\right>,

which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
p=2
. The space of singular vectors in
M_{1,1}^{3}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1}\right>
is spanned by

v_{3}=\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}).

The character of
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
is

\chi_{M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>}(z)=\chi_{M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)-(z+z^{3})\chi_{M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z).

The first terms of the character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+z^{2}+2z^{3}+\ldots)+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+2z^{2}+0\cdot z^{3}+\ldots).

Proof.

By Theorem
2.12
, in the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(S_{3})

M^{3}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})=3[\mathrm{Stand}]+2[\mathrm{Triv}],

and, as
\left<v_{1}\right>\cong M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Triv})[-1]
, we similarly get

[\left<v_{1}\right>^{3}]=[M^{2}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Triv})]=[\mathrm{Stand}]+[\mathrm{Triv}].

Consequently,

M^{3}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1}\right>=2[\mathrm{Stand}]+[\mathrm{Triv}].

By Lemma
3.9
we only need to inspect the
\mathrm{Triv}
isotypic component of
M^{3}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1}\right>
for singular vectors, which by Theorem
2.12
has a basis
v_{3}=\overline{\sigma_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})
.
We calculate:

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(v_{3})

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{2}}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})+\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+

\displaystyle+\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}

\displaystyle=(\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}})\otimes\overline{x_{2}}

\displaystyle=(\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}})v_{1}\in\left<v_{1}\right>,

\displaystyle D_{y_{2}-y_{3}}(v_{3})

\displaystyle=s_{1}.D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}s_{1}.(v_{3})

\displaystyle=s_{1}.D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(v_{3}+\overline{\sigma_{2}}v_{1})\in\left<v_{1}\right>.

So,
v_{3}
is singular in
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1}\right>
(though not in
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
).

We have
\left<v_{1}\right>\cong M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Triv})[-1]
,
\left<v_{3}\right>\cong M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Triv})[-3]
, and
\left<v_{1}\right>\cap\left<v_{3}\right>=0
(by direct computation). This implies the claims about the characters.

∎

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
p=2
. There are no singular vectors in
M^{4}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
.
Hence, the first terms of the character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+z^{2}+2z^{3}+z^{4}+\ldots)+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+2z^{2}+2z^{4}+\ldots).

Proof.

Assume there was a singular vector in
M^{4}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
. By Lemma
3.9
it is in a
\mathrm{Stand}
isotypic component. It generates a nonzero submodule
V
of
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
, which is a quotient of
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})[-4]
and thus
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})[-4]
is a quotient of
V
. Thus,
V^{6}
contains at least as many copies of
\mathrm{Triv}
as
L_{1,1}^{2}(\mathrm{Stand}),
which is
2
by Lemma
3.22
. However,
V
is a submodule of
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
which also by Lemma
3.22
contains no copies of
\mathrm{Triv}
. This is impossible, so we conclude there is no singular vector in
M^{4}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
.

∎

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
p=2
. The space of singular vectors in
M^{5}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
is spanned by

v_{5}=\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}).

Hence, the first terms of the character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+z^{2}+2z^{3}+z^{4}+z^{5}+\ldots)+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+2z^{2}+2z^{4}+z^{5}+\ldots).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
, any singular vectors in degree
5
are of type
\mathrm{Triv}
, so we only examine this. Using Theorem
2.12
, a basis for the
\mathrm{Triv}
component of
M^{5}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
is

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}v_{1}

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}^{2}(\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}\otimes\overline{x_{2}})=\overline{\sigma_{2}}v_{3}

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}).

Here
A,C
span submodules, and
B,D
their extensions. Quotienting by
\left<v_{1},v_{3}\right>
annihilates
A
and
B
. The only submodule of type
\mathrm{Triv}
is spanned by
C
, so we set
v_{5}=C
and check that is singular:

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(v_{5})

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{2}}\overline{\sigma_{2}}v_{1}\in\left<v_{1}\right>

\displaystyle D_{y_{2}-y_{3}}(v_{5})

\displaystyle=s_{1}.\overline{x_{2}}\overline{\sigma_{2}}v_{1}=\overline{x_{1}}\overline{\sigma_{2}}v_{1}\in\left<v_{1}\right>.

This proves that
v_{5}
is singular, and shows that the first terms of the character of
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>
are as stated in the Lemma. It remains to show that
D
is not singular, so we calculate

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(D)

\displaystyle=\overline{\sigma_{2}}\overline{x_{1}}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{\sigma_{3}}v_{1}\notin\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>.

∎

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
p=2
. There are no singular vectors in
M^{6}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>
.
Hence, the first terms of the character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+z^{2}+2z^{3}+z^{4}+z^{5}+z^{6}+\ldots)+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+2z^{2}+2z^{4}+z^{5}+0\cdot z^{6}+\ldots).

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
3.23
. A singular vector in
M_{1,1}^{6}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>
would be of type
\mathrm{Stand}
and generate a submodule of
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>
with one copy of
\mathrm{Stand}
in degree
7
, which is impossible as
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>
has no copies of
\mathrm{Stand}
in degree
7
.

∎

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
p=2
. The space of singular vectors in
M^{7}_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>
is spanned by

v_{7}=\overline{\sigma_{2}}\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}).

Hence, the first terms of the character of
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
are

\chi_{L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})}(z)=[\mathrm{Stand}](1+z+z^{2}+2z^{3}+z^{4}+z^{5}+z^{6}+0\cdot z^{7}+\ldots)+

+[\mathrm{Triv}](z+2z^{2}+2z^{4}+z^{5}+0\cdot z^{6}+0\cdot z^{7}+\ldots).

Proof.

The
\mathrm{Triv}
component of
M_{1,1}^{7}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>
has a basis

v_{7}
(by repeated use of Theorem
2.12
and
2.10
). To see this vector is singular we calculate

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(v_{7})

\displaystyle=D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{\sigma_{2}}\overline{\sigma_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\otimes\overline{x_{2}}))

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{1}}v_{5}+(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+\overline{x_{2}}^{3})v_{3}\in\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\right>.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.19
.

By Lemmas
3.20
,
3.21
,
3.22
,
3.23
,
3.24
,
3.25
and
3.26
the module
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5},v_{7}\right>
has no singular vectors in degrees up to
7
, and thus coincides with the module
L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
in those degrees. On the other hand Lemma
3.26
shows that this module is
0
in degree
7
, so it is zero in all higher degrees as well, and we conclude
M_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})/\left<v_{1},v_{3},v_{5},v_{7}\right>=L_{1,1}(\mathrm{Stand})
, and its character is as stated. The Hilbert polynomial follows directly.

∎

4.
Irreducible representations of
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
in characteristic
3

As explained in Section
2.1
, over an algebraically closed field
\Bbbk
of characteristic
3
the irreducible representations of
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
are
L_{t,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
and
L_{t,c}(\mathrm{Sign})
. We continue to work with the spanning set
\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{2}},\overline{x_{3}}
of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
satisfying
\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{3}}=0
and the basis
\overline{x_{1}},\overline{x_{2}}
. The aim of the section is to prove the following theorem. Recall that by Remark
2.9
,

\chi_{S^{(3)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)=[\mathrm{Triv}](1+z+2z^{2}+z^{3}+z^{4})+[\mathrm{Sign}](z+z^{2}+z^{3}).

Theorem 4.1
.

The characters of the irreducible representation
L_{t,c}(\tau)
of the rational Cherednik algebra
H_{t,c}(S_{3},\mathfrak{h})
over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
3
, for any
c,t
and
\tau
, are given by the following table.

p=3

\mathrm{Triv}

\mathrm{Sign}

t=0
, all
c

[\mathrm{Triv}]

[\mathrm{Sign}]

t=1
, all
c

\chi_{S^{(3)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)

\chi_{S^{(3)}\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)\cdot[\mathrm{Sign}]

The corresponding Hilbert polynomials are:

p=3

\mathrm{Triv}

\mathrm{Sign}

t=0
, all
c

1

1

t=1
, all
c

\left(\frac{1-z^{3}}{1-z}\right)^{2}

\left(\frac{1-z^{3}}{1-z}\right)^{2}

[
DSb
]

In all cases the generators of
J_{t,c}(\tau)
are and given explicitly in the lemmas below.

Proof.

The description of
L_{t,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
is given in:

•

for
t=0
and any
c
, in Lemma
2.19
;

•

for
t=1
and any
c
, in Lemma
4.2
.

The analogous descriptions of the irreducible representation
L_{t,c}(\mathrm{Sign})
can be deduced from the description of
L_{t,-c}(\mathrm{Triv})
and Lemma
2.15
. Note that the generators of
J_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
and the Hilbert polynomials of
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
for generic

are known from
[

.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
p=3
, and
t=1
and
c\in\Bbbk
be arbitrary. Vectors
\overline{x_{1}}^{3}
and
\overline{x_{2}}^{3}
in
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
are singular. The quotient
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>
is irreducible, equal to
L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
and has the character and the Hilbert polynomial

\displaystyle\chi_{L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{Triv}](1+z+2z^{2}+z^{3}+z^{4})+[\mathrm{Sign}](z+z^{2}+z^{3})

\displaystyle h_{L_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})}(z)

\displaystyle=1+2z+3z^{2}+2z^{3}+z^{4}=\left(\frac{1-z^{3}}{1-z}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

Let us first show that
\overline{x_{1}}^{3}
and
\overline{x_{2}}^{3}
are singular in
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})
.
We compute

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3})

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{y_{1}-y_{3}}-c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(12)}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}-c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(23)}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}-2c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(13)}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{3}}}\right)(\overline{x_{1}}^{3})

\displaystyle=-c\cdot\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+0+2\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+2\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{3}}+2\overline{x_{3}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-c\cdot\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+2\overline{x_{1}}^{2}-2\overline{x_{1}}^{2}-2\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+2\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+4\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+2\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-c\cdot\left(3\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+3\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+3\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right)=0

\displaystyle D_{y_{2}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}})

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{y_{1}-y_{3}}+c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(12)}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}-2c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(23)}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}-c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(13)}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{3}}}\right)(\overline{x_{1}}^{3})

\displaystyle=c\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}-2\cdot 0-\overline{x_{1}}^{2}-\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{3}}-\overline{x_{3}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=c\left(\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{1}}^{2}-2\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right)=0.

By symmetry,

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(12).D_{y_{2}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3})=0

\displaystyle D_{y_{2}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{2}}^{3})

\displaystyle=(12).D_{y_{1}-y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{3})=0.

So,
\overline{x_{1}}^{3}
and
\overline{x_{2}}^{3}
are singular.

Next, we calculate the character of
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>
. This is easiest to do in the basis
a_{+}=\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}
,
a_{-}=\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}
of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, noting that
\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>=\left<a_{+}^{3},a_{-}^{3}\right>
. The quotient
M^{1}_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<a_{+}^{3},a_{-}^{3}\right>
has a basis

\left\{a_{+},a_{-},a_{-}^{2},a_{-}a_{+},a_{+}^{2},a_{-}^{2}a_{+},a_{-}a_{+}^{2},a_{-}^{2}a_{+}^{2}\right\}.

This and the fact that
[\Bbbk a_{+}]=[\mathrm{Triv}]
and
[\Bbbk a_{-}]=[\mathrm{Sign}]
let us show that the character and the Hilbert polynomial are as stated.

It remains to show that
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>
is irreducible. We do so in a way similar to the proof of Lemma
3.5
, using the fact that the module
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>\cong S\mathfrak{h}^{*}/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>
is also a Frobenius algebra. We work with the basis
\left\{\overline{x_{1}}^{a}\overline{x_{2}}^{b}\mid 0\leq a,b<3\right\}
.

Assume that
U
is a nonzero graded submodule of
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>
, and
v\in U
some nonzero homogeneous vector. Multiplying by a nonzero constant if needed,
v
can be written as

v=\overline{x_{1}}^{a}\overline{x_{2}}^{b}+\sum_{a^{\prime}>a}\alpha_{a^{\prime}}\overline{x_{1}}^{a^{\prime}}\overline{x_{2}}^{a+b-a^{\prime}}

for some
a,b
with
0\leq a,b<3
and
\alpha_{a^{\prime}}\in\Bbbk
.
As
U
is a subrepresentation and using
\overline{x_{1}}^{3}=0
, we get

\overline{x_{1}}^{2-a}\overline{x_{2}}^{2-b}v=\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+\sum_{a^{\prime}>a}\alpha_{a}^{\prime}\overline{x_{1}}^{2+a^{\prime}-a}\overline{x_{2}}^{2+a-a^{\prime}}=\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}

is in
U
. Then
U
also contains

D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{2}D_{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}}^{2}\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2}.

(Note this makes sense as in characteristic
3
we have
Y=y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}\in\mathfrak{h}
.)

Using
\left<Y,\alpha_{s}\right>=0
for all
\alpha_{s}
so
D_{Y}=\partial_{Y}
, we calculate

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{2}D_{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}}^{2}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=2D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{2}D_{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=2D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{2}\left(2\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+2\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{2}\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right).

We now use the fact that in characteristic
3
we have
2(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2})=\overline{\sigma_{2}}
and that
D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}(\overline{\sigma_{2}})=\partial_{y_{1}-y_{2}}(\overline{\sigma_{2}})
to get

\displaystyle D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}^{2}D_{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}}^{2}(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}\overline{x_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=2D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}\partial_{y_{1}-y_{2}}\left(\overline{x_{1}}^{2}+\overline{x_{1}}\overline{x_{2}}+\overline{x_{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}\left(2\overline{x_{1}}+\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{1}}-2\overline{x_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2D_{y_{1}-y_{2}}\left(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2\left(\partial_{y_{1}-y_{2}}-2c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(12)}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}}}-c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(13)}{\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{3}}}+c\frac{\mathrm{id}-(23)}{\overline{x_{2}}-\overline{x_{3}}}\right)(\overline{x_{1}}-\overline{x_{2}})

\displaystyle=2\left(1+1-2c\cdot 2-c\cdot 1+c\cdot(-1)\right)

\displaystyle=4-12c=1.

So,
1\in U
. However,
1
generates the module
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>
, and consequently
U
is the whole module. This shows that
M_{1,c}(\mathrm{Triv})/\left<\overline{x_{1}}^{3},\overline{x_{2}}^{3}\right>
is irreducible.
∎

5.
Auxiliary computations using the rescaled Young basis

For the rest of the paper the characteristic of the field
\Bbbk
is
p>3
. So, the category of representations of
S_{3}
is semisimple, we can realise
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
as a subrepresentation of
V^{*}
with the basis

b_{+}=x_{1}+x_{2}-2x_{3},\quad b_{-}=x_{1}-x_{2},

from Section
2.3
, and we will be using the bases of
M_{t,c}(\tau)
from Theorems
2.4
and
2.6
. To manipulate those bases we will first need to know the action of all reflections on
b_{+},b_{-}
, which can be easily calculated to be:

\displaystyle(12).b_{+}=b_{+},\quad

\displaystyle(12).b_{-}=-b_{+}

(6)

\displaystyle(23).b_{+}=\frac{-b_{+}+3b_{-}}{2},\quad

\displaystyle(23).b_{-}=\frac{b_{+}+b_{-}}{2}

\displaystyle(13).b_{+}=\frac{-b_{+}-3b_{-}}{2},\quad

\displaystyle(13).b_{-}=\frac{-b_{+}+b_{-}}{2}.

The main advantages of the basis
b_{+},b_{-}
for
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is that it lets us use Theorems
2.4
and
2.6
to write a bases of each Verma modules, itself good because:

(1)

it is compatible with the decomposition into
S_{3}
representations, so we can use Lemma
2.1
to reduce the space in which we look for singular vectors;

(2)

every vector in this basis is either
S_{2}
invariant or anti-invariant, enabling us to use Lemma
2.20
;

(3)

calculating Dunkl operators in this basis is manageable. Namely, each vectors in it is a product of an
S_{3}
invariant and one of a small number of fixed low degree expressions in
b_{+},b_{-}
. The differential part of the Dunkl operator satisfies the Leibniz rule so is easy to apply to products, and the difference part of the Dunkl operator is linear with respect to invariants so will only need to be calculated on a small number of low degree vectors.

The main disadvantage of the basis
b_{+},b_{-}
and resulting bases Verma modules is that multiplication (except by a symmetric polynomial) is more involved. We gather all tedious multiplication formulas among
b_{+},b_{-},(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2}),2b_{+}b_{-}
and
q
we will need for our computations of singular vectors later into one lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

The following identities hold in
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
:

\displaystyle b_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle=-6\sigma_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle b_{-}^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\sigma_{2}+\frac{1}{6}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle b_{+}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle=6\cdot q-6\cdot\sigma_{2}b_{-}

\displaystyle b_{+}\cdot(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle=54\cdot\sigma_{3}+6\cdot\sigma_{2}b_{+}

\displaystyle b_{-}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle=18\cdot\sigma_{3}-2\cdot\sigma_{2}b_{+}

\displaystyle b_{-}\cdot(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle=-6\cdot q-6\cdot\sigma_{2}b_{-}

\displaystyle(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=72\cdot\sigma_{2}^{2}-108\cdot\sigma_{3}b_{+}-6\cdot\sigma_{2}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle=108\cdot\sigma_{3}b_{-}+6\cdot\sigma_{2}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle(2b_{+}b_{-})^{2}

\displaystyle=24\cdot\sigma_{2}^{2}+36\cdot\sigma_{3}b_{+}+2\cdot\sigma_{2}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle q\cdot b_{+}

\displaystyle=-9\cdot\sigma_{3}b_{-}-1\cdot\sigma_{2}\cdot 2\cdot b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle q\cdot b_{-}

\displaystyle=3\cdot\sigma_{3}b_{+}+\frac{1}{3}\cdot\sigma_{2}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})\cdot q

\displaystyle=-12\cdot\sigma_{2}^{2}b_{-}+9\cdot\sigma_{3}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle 2b_{+}b_{-}\cdot q

\displaystyle=4\cdot\sigma_{2}^{2}b_{+}-3\cdot\sigma_{3}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle q^{2}

\displaystyle=-27\sigma_{3}^{2}-4\sigma_{2}^{3}.

Proof.

Direct computation.
∎

When looking for singular vectors, we will make liberal use of Lemma
2.1
to reduce the space where we are looking for singular vectors, and Lemma

2.20
to reduce the task of finding
\cap_{y\in\mathfrak{h}}\mathrm{Ker}D_{y}
to the task of finding
\mathrm{Ker}D_{y_{1}}
. For that purpose, let us make
D_{y_{1}}
explicit as

D_{y_{1}}=t\partial_{y_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}-c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{id}-(12)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\otimes(12)-c\cdot\frac{id-(13)}{x_{1}-x_{3}}\otimes(13).

For any reflection
s
, any vector
v\in M_{t,c}(\tau)
or one of its quotients, and any
f\in(S\mathfrak{h}^{*})^{S_{3}}
we have

(7)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathrm{id}-s}{\alpha_{s}}\otimes s\right)(fv)=f\cdot\left(\frac{\mathrm{id}-s}{\alpha_{s}}\otimes s\right)(v).

Let us now calculate the action of the divided difference operators and the partial derivatives on all factors which appear in the bases of
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and for
M_{t,c}(\tau)
from Theorems
2.4
and
2.6
.

Lemma 5.2
.

The following identities hold in
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
:

\displaystyle\frac{id-(12)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}(b_{+})=0,\quad

\displaystyle\frac{id-(13)}{x_{1}-x_{3}}(b_{+})=3,

\displaystyle\frac{id-(12)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}(b_{-})=2,\quad

\displaystyle\frac{id-(13)}{x_{1}-x_{3}}(b_{-})=1,

\displaystyle\frac{id-(12)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})=0,\quad

\displaystyle\frac{id-(13)}{x_{1}-x_{3}}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})=3(-b_{+}+3b_{-}),

\displaystyle\frac{id-(12)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}(2b_{+}b_{-})=4b_{+},\quad

\displaystyle\frac{id-(13)}{x_{1}-x_{3}}(2b_{+}b_{-})=-b_{+}+3b_{-},

\displaystyle\frac{id-(12)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}(q)=-2\sigma_{2}-\frac{1}{3}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2}),\quad

\displaystyle\frac{id-(13)}{x_{1}-x_{3}}(q)=2\sigma_{2}-\frac{1}{6}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}.

Proof.

Direct computation.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

The following identities hold in
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(b_{+})=1,\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(b_{-})=1,

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})=-2b_{+}+6b_{-},\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(2b_{+}b_{-})=2b_{+}+2b_{-}

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{2})=\frac{-1}{6}b_{+}+\frac{-1}{2}b_{-},\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{3})=\frac{1}{36}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{1}{12}(2b_{+}b_{-}),

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(q)=\frac{-1}{4}

\displaystyle(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{1}{4}(2b_{+}b_{-}).

Proof.

Direct computation.
∎

Finally, in the attempt to express the value of the Dunkl operator
D_{y_{1}}
on a vector in our basis as a linear combination of basis vectors, we will often need the following computations.

Lemma 5.4
.

The following identities hold in
S\mathfrak{h}^{*}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot b_{+}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}+\frac{1}{12}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{-1}{4}(2b_{+}b_{-})

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{3})\cdot b_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\sigma_{3}+\frac{1}{6}\sigma_{2}b_{+}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2}b_{-}+\frac{1}{2}q

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot b_{-}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}+\frac{-1}{12}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{-1}{12}(2b_{+}b_{-})

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{3})\cdot b_{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\sigma_{3}+\frac{-1}{6}\sigma_{2}b_{+}+\frac{-1}{6}\sigma_{2}b_{-}+\frac{-1}{6}q

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle=-9\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{2}b_{+}+3\sigma_{2}b_{-}+3q

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{3})\cdot(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle=2\sigma_{2}^{2}-3\sigma_{3}b_{+}+9\sigma_{3}b_{-}-\frac{1}{6}\sigma_{2}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2}(2b_{+}b_{-})

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle=-9\sigma_{3}+\sigma_{2}b_{+}+\sigma_{2}b_{-}-q

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{3})\cdot 2b_{+}b_{-}

\displaystyle=2\sigma_{2}^{2}+3\sigma_{3}b_{+}+3\sigma_{3}b_{-}+\frac{1}{6}\sigma_{2}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{1}{6}\sigma_{2}(2b_{+}b_{-})

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot q

\displaystyle=\frac{-3}{2}\sigma_{3}b_{+}+\frac{3}{2}\sigma_{3}b_{-}+\frac{-1}{6}\sigma_{2}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{1}{6}\sigma_{2}(2b_{+}b_{-})

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}(\sigma_{3})\cdot q

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\sigma_{2}^{2}b_{+}\frac{-1}{3}\sigma_{2}^{2}b_{-}+\frac{-1}{4}\sigma_{3}(-b_{+}^{2}+3b_{-}^{2})+\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}(2b_{+}b_{-}).

Proof.

Direct computation.
∎

The generators of

To write the above characters explicitly, recall from Corollary

that

\chi_{S\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-z^{2})(1-z^{3})}\left([\mathrm{Triv}]+[\mathrm{Stand}](z+z^{2})+[\mathrm{Sign}]z^{3}\right).

We note that
[
DSa
]
already provide these characters and Hilbert polynomials in their Propositions 4.1 and 4.2, in much greater generality of
G(m,r,n)
. We do not use their results. Our main contribution is to give explicit singular vectors, and their results are reproved along the way. Other than for its general interest, these singular vectors are needed in Section

where we describe
L_{t,c}(\tau)
for special

- a case
[
